can always be chosen at DUTTON'S. Amongst the hundreds of different classes of goods we display, ranging from a 40 guinea fur coat to a modest pocket handkerchief, there is bound to be some article that is "just •

the very thing." We have numberless suggestions to offer in the way of practical, sensible, and serviceable gifts for every member of the family at home or abroad.

Every facility for comfortable -shop- ping is given, and we can guarantee your purchases to bear a perfect expression of quality combined with a marked superiority of wear in comparison with other goods at similar prices.

No matter how much or how little you spend satisfaction is assured. Now, in matters of dress we have a charming selection of Furs, Cos- tumes, Gowns, Millinery, Blouses, Silks, Laces, Hosiery, Lingerie, Gloves, Perfumes, and Novelties of every description.

In Furnishings, we offer exceptional value in Household Linens, Curtains, Carpets, Rugs, etc.

In Gentlemen's Outfitting, Overcoats, Trench Coats, Burberrys, Mufflers, Woollen Gloves Socks, Leather Waistcoats, Khaki Shirts, Handker- chiefs, and so on; all of which will make very acceptable presents for the soldier friend or relative.

In fact, DUTTON'S have the Gift you're looking for. Why not come and get it?

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1916.

PREPARING FOR THE GREAT FESTIVAL.

Christmas pay tails three weeks on Monday, and like all other great festivals it is looked forward to with eagerness for weeks ahead; To ' all of us it is more than a day on which, we cease work (excepting the cook, of course, and she is on bonus rates); otherwise it would be a mere Bank Holiday, and to Lord Avebury or some other mortal concerned rather with material than "religious or- -'ethicaCideas, we should have to ascribe the glory. Everyone knows why the Christmas festival was insti- tuted, and though the simplicity of its initiation has been added to, the genius of its origination has always been respected and adored in Christian countries. But that Christmas should be spent in the way common in later years is quite in accordance with religious propriety. The spirit of Rabeleas is kept without the gate, but that of Falstaff May be admitted. And it is. Signs- that Christmas is coming are every- where apparent. There are the calendar, the nip of winter, the glorious shops, the Army Pudding Fund, and the knocking of house doors by youngsters who want to know whether they may sing carols as paid artistes. If he has the nerve to go back in memory there is one man who will suffer disappointment this Christmas—the Kaiser. When war broke out he was sure he was going to have his Christ- mas dinner of 1914 in London, probablfwith the British Cabinet waiting on, and the nation pick- ing up the crumbs. If he had not been politely

TRADESMEN'S ENTERPRISE SURMOUNTS WAR CONDITIONS.

delayed on the way when he was walking—or was he aviating on the German eagle?—to Calais through Ypres, he might have been a self-invited guest in - Warrington by now. He might

===== 0002601_19161202_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTRASTS.

Through the war Christmas will, of cour& be different from that in peace times, but, hether they were at the Front or in the harbour of their homes, folks last year and the previous Christ- mas had many a happy story to narrate, and we have no need to anticipate that things will be much different this time. There are some rigid and disciplinary souls who since the outbreak of war have suspended their enjoyment of life— they are not to be found in the trenches. That is nothing but waste of life; cheerfulness in ad.

•versity' and strain is a maxim sanctioned by practical philosophers. We should have liked the lads with us, but it cannot be, and as long as we look after their wants while they are away we shall be doing our duty. As a period of recreation and pleasure Christmas is being recognised by the Government, for the Ministry of Munitions has announced that wherever possible there shall be a suspension of work for a few days. That Christmas Day is this year falling on a Monday is extremely satisfactory, for with Sunday and Boxing Day, a long week-end of rest is in prospect.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOLIDAYS.

If in these times Christmas should not be an occasion for repletion, it should, at any rate, be one for replenishment. And it is with this idea that the shopkeeners are presenting wonderful displays of goods in their windows. Now-a- nights every shop is a fairyland—the variegated colours of the shades round the lamps lend a charm and almost oriental aspect to the scene within, without being a blaze of colour, as in the times when Zeppelins seemed mere toys of amusement. As will be seen from the adver- tisements, Warrington shopkeepers are full of enterprise, catering amply for all K,ctions of the community—and if you ask for what they have not got they will soon put it on order for you.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
PEARS' ANNUAL FOR 1916.

Each year's issue of this most popular of Christmas Annuals seems to outstrip its prede- cessors in pictorial delight and literary attrac- tion, and the issue for 1916 stands out as a re- markable example of this progressive spirit. The special art features include three fine pres- entation plates, exquisitely executed reproduc- tions in colours and mezzogravure of works of great charm and beauty by J. Godward, G. Sheridan Knowles, R. 1.. and Chas. E. Brock, R. 1., each picture being eminently suitable for framing. The o 'er, too, is in itself an artistic fascination, forming a superb facsimile in colours of a drawing by Frank Dadd, R. 1., en- titled Home from the Wars, 1815." Another wonderfully spirited and highly humorous art contribution is a four page supple- ment in colours by John Hassan, R. 1., illustrat-: ing "An Old Time Christmas." The literary contents comprise a bright collection of stories and poems appropriate to the time and season by well-known writers, including a stirring war story by Boyd Cable, a picturesque tale of a Christmas of the long ago by Max Pemberton, a startling Zeppelin story by Fergus Hume, aril other tales and poems of humour and sentiment by 11. De Vere Stacpoole, Keble Howard and James Burnley, with illustrations in colours and tints by Frank Reynolds, R. 1., Joseph -Simpson, R.8.A., Stephen-Spurrier, R. 1., Cyrus Cuneo, R. 1., Charles Pears, and F. Leist, R.I. It is a production of the highest standard from every point of view.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUNAL AND SUBSTITUTES.

Taking up the list at. the Warrington Rural District Tribunal on Wednesday, the Chairman (Mr. George Mather) explained that the cases for consideration that afternoon came up for careful revision, on the application of the mili- tary representative. Mr. William Furness, military representative, said the military would undertake to supply a substitute before the man was taken off. As far as the military were concerned there would be no decrease in agricultural labour. Mr. Bridge said that it would be difficult to get substitutes for expert farm hands.

Mr. J. Ball said the substitution, ae far as he could see, was to be from German civilians. The first case on the list was that of a man aged 27, single, employed as a horseman by a Rixton farmer, and the application of the mili- tary representative: was on. the: ground ,that pot- withstanding the man's occupation was a certi- fied one, it *as no longer necessary in the national interest that he should continue in civil employment. The Tribunal .decided that the man should be allowed to stop on the firm until a fit man could be substituted for, him. •

Applicant expresed some doubt as to the mili- tary being able to provide a satisfactory sub- stitute.

The Chairman said that a committee had been appointed to see that these men were equal to the that they were going to perform. Mr. Furness said that as far as possible the military would give a man equally skilled for the one that was taken. It was up to farmers to help the men to do it, and to suffer any little inconvenience from time to time.

In a number of other eases the military repre- sentative's appeal was also upheld, but the men will not be taken until suitable substitutes are found.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1916. tans toot (Mint STAG E LAND. ramiam

What Managers Think Of Their Own Shows.

Royal Court. Instead of wrting about the entertainments- this week, I decided to invite the managers to record what they thought about their own shows. So being at the Royal Court when the idea occurred to me, I first mentioned it to Mr. Harry .Warburton, asking him to sit down at his typewriter in his snug little office, and cul- tivate the muse. He obliged.

"I wonder what 'Foyer' expects me to say," he inquired on the typewriter. "However, here goes—or, as Miss Madge Grey says, Now do something.' I never remember in my eight years at the Royal Court Theatre, such hearty laughter and such generous appreciatron of talent on our stage. 'Our Kid,' stands right out as a laughter-maker, and my advice to the public of Warrington, who may be suffering from war strain, is to see Madge Grey in this rollicking farce to-night.

" I think the company is a very excellent one. To of , the male members have been on active service—I, refer to Mr. John Kelvin and Mr. Claude Seaton. They are naturally so proud of their Army service that they wear the gold braid on their sleeves both on and off the stage on every suit they possess. Miss Grey considers it a great honour to have as principal supporters such men. •

"What strikes one so forcibly about Miss Grey is her versatility. Either as a servant girl, or as "the Kid "—and -what a• charming innocent

little bunch of mischief she-is—or as an actress of refinement, she is convincing.

"It is some two year. . since Miss Grey ap- peared at this theatre in 'Our Kid,' and she has been on tour with this very successful comedy in the meantime. 43-.„ only regret is that so many people have been unable to gain _admission to the theatre this week, and I think I can promise a return ,visit next year

"I. hope I am not giving any secret away when I tell you that Miss Denise Ainsley is a .daughter of Miss Grey, . I am sure she has charmed the vast audiences this week with her.

•lightful personality. singing and dancing. She has certa44 inherited ".her mother's ability as an entertainer.

"Our Kid' cheers one. There is not a dull moment—singing, mimicry (on a high plane), wit, and beautiful dancing are the order all along the line,"

"Tanks. You have pitched the key just right," I said, when pocketting his MSS.

That Mr. Raynor's talented Repertoire Com- pany are paying us another visit next week is cause for enthusiasm. Warrington knows these people well as producers of the best plays. Miss Ennis Lawson again heads the bill.

The plays to be produced are of first-class quality. They are: "Camille,". Monday matinee and Saturday night; " Nell Gwynne " (first time produced by the Raynor Company), Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday nights and Thursday matinee; by special request, "Lady Hunt- worth's experiment," on Thursday night; and " The Dancing Girl" on Friday night

Mother's Secret.

" pEOPLE wonder sometimes how I manage to keep, so happy and strong with all the work to do at home. I do as Dad does—l make Rowntree's Elect Cocoa my stand-by. Whenever I feel a little tired I make myself a cup of Rowntree's. It's as refreshing as sunshine, and it's wonder- ful the way it puts new life into you. So I always keep a tin in thikupboard. As Dad says, 'Home wouldn't be Home without Rowntree's Elect Cocoa.'

And it would do you good to see how bonny the children keep on it. I love to watch their little faces glow with happiness when they taste the delicious Rowntree Flavour."

===== 0002601_19161202_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEER FOOD SUPPLY CORPS.

Sir,—The food question is becoming serious. Every means should be used to increase our supply. There is land about Warrington,. which, though used for cattle grazing, would make good arable land. There are many men outside the local Training Corps who might form a semi-military corps for the cultivation of potatoes. Their military drill might be in- cidental to the maximum production of vege- tables. The temporary use of the land Might be acquired by the corps, and the proceeds of the sale, after deducting expenses, divided amongst the men who had devoted their time to increas- ing the food supply. Many men who have had a period of drill with a Training Corps might join in a useful work such as the one suggested. It should be possible for each corps to do some- thing in the way of increasing the food supply, as a part of its activities. The experiment of getting a comparatively large number of men working in a methodical way would be both in- teresting and .useful. This is no time to stand as an amused onlooker; it is the time for man- fully pulling one's full weight for the national good. Are Englishmen capable of spontaneous and co-operate effort to help themselves in the present crisis? This is one way of answering in the affirmative.

FINE CONCERT

By The Warrington Musical Society.

Perhaps two special attractions, were princi- pally _responsible op. Saturday .evening. 'tor the magnificent audience which filled the Parr •Hall on the occasion of the grand concert given by the 'Warrington Musical Society in aid of local War Funds—Lady Crosfield at the pianoforte, and Miss Astra Desmond, principal contralto Royal Carl Rosa Company. Although admir- ably equipped for the interpretation of com- positions requiring, delicacy of treatment and a. fine knowledge Of technique, Lady Crosfield ap- parently favours morceaux where soulful har- monies are predominant, and her remarkable taste and delicacy of expression may be more fully displayed. • While the difficulties of grieg's (First Movement) Concerto . were easily over- come, her later contribution S reached the, hearts. of her audience, .being more 'conclusively marked, as they were, by the greater appealing qualities just mentioned,. If Miss Astra. Des- mond had been named Astrea one could have found a pleasing metaphor in the evening star whose mellow radiance suggests the delicious fullness of some of her notes. She threw•a vast amount of feeling into the Aria from 4` La Favorita," and clearly interpreted the opposi- tion of fine sentiment and dramatic fervour of Donizetti by her singing of "0 Mio Fernando." She was entrancing in Sanderson's . delightful song, The Hills of Donegal," and the charm- ing composition by Phillips, "Sing, joyous bird." The presence of such an adeomplished accompanist as Mr. W. H. Payton at the piano needs no comment to Warrington readers. Whether, the preparation was great or small, the members of the society in chorus and orches- tra attacked a programme of giugh charm and variety with singular success. In common with many societies of the kind, the Warrington Musical must have suffered sadly through the war; but the unavoidable lack of numbers was well atoned for by refined singing and most capable instrumental work.. Mr. Prank •H. Crossley, as conductor,' brought his musicians through with considerable skill, and Mr. R. H. Pearson was a most efficient leader of the orchestra. Donizetti was in special favour, the opening chorus being that distinctive item from "Don Pasquale "—" 0, • April night." Sir Ed- ward Elgar's latest composition, "For the Fallen," was sung with much discretion and succeSs, considering the special difficulties offered by this master in composition. Laurence Binyon's words are singularly appropriate, and give exceptional opportunity for musical colour. The poem progresses with rapid changes of feel- ing and sound. One fine verse was most appeal- ing:

"They shall grow not old, as we that are left grow old; Age shall not weary them, nor the years con- demn. At the going down of the sun and in the morning We will remember them."

Miss Brough took the soprano solo most effec- tively. "The Little Drummer—Rub-a-dub-dub" (Kappey) was excellhtly rendered, and H. J. Taylar's Empire song, "Grey Mother of. Mighty Nations," made a vigorous finale to a. prograniMe of conspicuous merit. As an overture the orchestra played Gounod's " Mirella" and two pieces by Coleridge Taylor, " Demande et- Re- ponse " and "La Caprice. de Nauvette," con- -stituted,a pleasing interlude., An excellent work, of .orchestration was the 'Performance of Sulli- van's incidental music to "henry VIII.." brim- ming over with the dainty effects of this brilli- ant composer. Miss Emily Taylor rendered the quaint snug (with chorus) "Youth will needs have dalliance" in a most charming manner. The Warrington Musical Society are to be con- gratulated on: this their opening concert of the present season.

THE LATCHFORD CADET CORPS.

ORDERS. Parades.

To-morrow (Sunday).---Churclt Parade; fall-in at 10 a.m. prompt. Service at 11 a.m. A good parade is expected. Tuesday and Friday.—Physical and Company Drill, at 7.30 prompt. Orderlies.—Lance-Corporal G. ifackwelk and Cadet A. Bates.

J. B. STAIZKEY, Commanding Officer.

Mr. Will A. Jackson, the popular Hippo- drome manager, courteously declined to have his own ideas about this week's show set out in type. "I am not a critic," he said, "and .I leave that work to •others. What else could I say except that everything is splendid?" Mr. Jackson was very busy, and was evidently thinking more particularly about the monster pudding matinee:. No :one would credit Mr. Jackson's statement that he .is not a critic. It would be hard to find a manager with a keener appreciation of real talent.

One of the funniest burlesque jugglers on the stage is , undoubtedly Zellini, whose comical ideas keep his audience in roars of laughter. Beyond question, his 'performing dogs take the dog biscuit. He styles himself the "Human Chimney," but it should not be forgotten that while there is only one vapour exit in a chim- ney, Zellini emits smoke from half a dozen apparent apertures. ,

Brenne and Erard are still one more pair of that increasing description of entertainers—a girl with a voice and a piano with a man at it. But ,there is a long distance between "This" and visa Clarice Mayne, aruj" That Hector and 'Miss Lolletta are fine equilibrists, and in-' traduce a very clever dog, who has a distinctly acrobatic turn.

De Busse with his piano-accordion--a novel in- strument of remarkably sweet and powerful tone—is a very welcome turn. After a well- executed operatic selection, he plays a number of popular choruses, to which his hearers readily supply the words. There is a mutual musical effort with which everyone appears to be highly delighted.

Cole Cliffe, Yorkshire comedian; Clifton and Miss Daisy, one-wheel wonders; and The Hil- tons in, a comedy melange, are most entertain- ing. The Hippodrome Orchestra supplies an- other little .treat this week. Mr. J. Crabtree plays 'a piccolo solo, "The Linnet" (F. Brockett), with easy skill and pleasing effect.

Next week we will have Cooper Mitchell, the versatile entertainer, in song and story at the piaini; the Tokiwa Family, a genuine company of Japanese artistes, with gorgeous apparel and effects; and the Benedetti Brothers, Italian acrobatic and musical comedians. Wal,Mars- den, the Lancashire Lad, should be a special draw, and the house will doubtless be crowded 'every night to discover if possible the secret of Allan Shay:, the money-making wizard.

Palace Theatre, Manchester. .. A big water scene and a real harlequinade are notable features of "Cinderella," the "super- Pantomime," which is to be produced at the Manchester Palace Theatre for six weeks,.cora- mencing December 10. An unprecedented array of talent, ,even for the Palace, has been en- gaged, including Clarice Mayne, Harry Weldon, Daisy Burrell, Bert Errol, Arthur Aiston, Daisy Taylor, Haidee Roselle, Bartand Bart, and the: Read troupe of pantomimists •. Mr. Gus- Sohlke, the producer, has manyewon- derful productions to his credit,. 'but " Cinder, ella " promises to be his most striking success in the •way of spectacular and novel effects. There will; be two performances daily—once nightly and every afternoon. _

"WOMAN AT HOME,"

The "Woman at. Tlome and Girl's Realm" Christmas number is a wonderful collection of good things. There ar.4 delightful stories by J'. J. Bell. Annie wan, Mary Stuart Boyd, and Mrs. de Horne Vaizey, a fine long instalment of L. Allen Harker's Charming serial, and a, short poem that is really a gem of its kind, by Patrick Mac Gill. Among the articles we find a stirring and thoughtful contribution from Harold Bebgie, entitled "After death." Turn- ing to the lighter side. of things, there is an amiably sarcastic article on women's dress, illus- trated with Lewis Baumer's pictures from "Punch," a contribution from "Lady Mary" deseriptive of "Possible Brides for the Prince of Wales," and an extremely interesting article called "The Triumph of Personality." There is a fine set of fashion pictures also in colour, a number of suggestions for Christmas presents, a splendid' new portrait of Miss Mary Macarthur, and all the usual features more than usually well presented.

At Tuesday's Borough Police Court. Albert Rutter, of 3, Scotland-road, was prosecuted by P.C. Taylor for a breach of the .recently-made regulations with regard to the earlier closing of shops. • Chief Constable Nicholls said that this was the first prosecution they had had under the recent Order, and the prosecution would not have been initiated if it had not been for the action of .the defendant, the defendant's wife, or.;stirne one.ja,thi,,k, shop. A little 'girl named Jones; 13. yeafg-dt ag-e; was 'seen currying out two boxes of matches after eight o'clock, and the constable learned that she had purchased them in defendant's shop. Subsequently the child was told to tell a certain story .to the police to prevent proceedings from being taken. Constable Taylor gave evidence and said that the, little .girl, when he saw her later, said that she had purchased the matches at six o'clock, but there were none in the shop and she went for them at eight o'clock. It was found iiiat the child had been asked to tell this story to the police. Defendant was fined 2s. CA.

A SEPARATION ORDER.

Mrs. Annie Elwell, 227, Liverpool-road; sum- moned her husband, Joseph Elwell, of 22, Pick. mere-street, for desertion, at the Borough Police Court on Thursday,' and was granted a separa- tion order, with 30s. a week alimony. Mr. A. Browne, for the complainant, said ahe was exceedingly sorry the case had not Wen settled outside the Police Court It was only a question of amount. They thought that as Mr. Elwell was head furnaceman at the works of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., earning about £8 per week, he could afford to give his •*.ife:' 30s. a week and rent free, instead of 205., which he had offered. If he would not accept these conditions he thought Mrs. Elwell was entitled to the full amount of 1.2. They had had no money from him for eight weeks. .Mr. T. S. Steel, for the defendant, said that Mr. Elwell very much regretted that the case had had to come before the Bench, but his wife was a very jealous woman, and Mr. ' Elwell had had to leave her after' standing a great deal from her. He left her .in the hope that it would put his wife into a proper state of mind, when he hoped .to return to her. His work varied from time to time, his earnings last year being £175. Regarding the statement that he had given his wife no money for eight weeks, Mr. Steel said that the defendant had allowed his wife £1 per week, with rent free, and had paid gas, milk .and meat bills whilst living together. The position now was exactly the same, excepting that during the last eight weeks She had not received the .21, but she had not had him to keep.- He (Mr. Steel) submitted that the offer of ..21 per week was-ample.

Did not Wish It.

William Wilberforce, the slave liberator, had a sister who was a hustler. She hustled for at the hnstings and succeeded in get- ting him elected to Parliament. On one occa- sion, when she had concluded her stump speech, some enthusiasts in the crowd shouted : "Miss Wilberforce for ever !" The lady stepped forward.

'Gentlemen, I thank you," she said, "but believe me, I do not wish to be Miss Wilberforce for ever."

Astonishing incredulity on the part of many women who have visited so-called fortune- tellers for the purpose of having revealed to them their future careers, especially in the matrimonial phase, was disclosed at the Bor- ough Police Court on Thursday, when two married women, Mary Bolton, 39, Pinners- brow, and Martha' Moorcroft, 36, West-street, were fined ..-05 each. The first case was that of Mrs. Bolton, who was charged with three offences on the 12th, 19th and 25th October.

The first witness (a lady) stated that when she visited the defendant's house on the 12t'3 there were two or three more women there. She was told that her husband was very ill and that she would draw some money from the Government. He was in a galloping consump- tion, and he "would gallop like a b— horse until he dropped." He would die in five weeks, she said. There was a dark young man abroad who thought a lot about witness. She was then told to wish, saying her wish would certainly be granted. On a later visit she was told she would be a widow in three weeks. Another witness, who had been visiting the defenesnt's house for several• years for the purpose of having her fortune indicated, said she was told she would be married in three weas and that there would be a death in Lhe farrily. She was asked to bring a bottle of Nvh:sky the next time she came. - A third witness (a married woman) was told she was a widow, that her husband was killed at Mons, and that she would marry again in five days, weeks, or months—it would certainly be "in the fives."

Defendant expressed her sorrow; saying she had nine children to keep. She had told one of the witness's fortune for years, "just for a hit of fan." • •

The defendant Moorcroft was charged with similar offences on two different dates. ,The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) said the Bench had no sympathy with the folly and ignorance of the people with whom the defend- ants had traded.

"FOLLY AND IGNORANCE."

===== 0002601_19161202_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
"WHOSE DEBTORS WE ARE."

They held, against the storms of fate, In war's tremendous game, A little land inviolate Within a world aflame.

They looked on scarred and ruined lands, On shell-wrecked fields forlorn, And gave to us, with open hands, Full fields of yellow corn;

The silence wrought in wood and stone, Whose aisles our fathers trod; The pines that stand apart, "alone, Like sentinels of God;

The stars that guard the quiet night, .Pin-pricked against thl blue; The wind-swept dawn whose tranquil light Is mirrored in the dew.

_ With generous hands they paid the, price Unconscious of the cost, But we must gauge the sacrifice By all that. they have lost.

The joy of young adventurous ways, Of keen and 'undimmed sight, The eager trathp through sunny days, The dreamless sleep,of night, - •

The happy hours that come and go In youth's utftiring quest. They gave, because ,they willed it so„ With some light-hearted jest.

No lavish love of future years, No passionate regret, No gift of sacrifice or tears Can ever pay the debt.'

—" Punch."

Age Not Involved.

"How are you to-day, John?" said a land- lord to one of his tenants whom he met in the street. "Vera weel, sir, vera „wee'," answered John, in his usual way, " if it wisna for the rheumatics in my richt leg." " Ah, well, John, be thankful, for there is no mistake you are getting old like the rest of us, and old age does npt come alone." "Auld age,, sir!" replied John.. "I wonder to hear ye: Auld age has naothiag to dae wi't. Here's my ither leg jist as ault, and it's soond and soople yet."

WAR CERTIFICATES FOR BABIES.

The National War Savings Committee re- ports that the number of War Saving Certifi- cates sold during the week ending November,. 18th was 1,536,242, making a total to date or 48,148,658. For Six per Cent. Post OffiCe Exche- quer Bonds there were 26,000 applications,. the Bonds taken being £1,100,000 in value, making the total value of the Bonds taken £7,200,000.

A Deptford doctor has a scheme on hand in connection with a "baby club" to purchase for , each infant of two or three months old a War Savings Certificate (15s. 6d.) to be held by the doctor till .the child is one year old. If the child dies before that age the certificate will 1, return to the doctor. If the child lives over twelve months, and dies before the certificate matures, the certificate becomes the property of the parents. If the child lives until the certi- ficate matures, the certificate is its own. The only condition is that the child shall be brought to the club every three months for examination to show that it is properly fed. cleaned, and clothed.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CROPS.

The average yield of potatoes in England and Wales is estimated at 5.85 tons per acre, or just one-third of a ten below the yield of 1915 and the ten-years average; with a somewhat reduced acreage, the total production of two and a half million tons is about 350,000 tons less than last year, but only 180,000 tons below the average. Turnips and swedes have produced almost 13 million tons, which is nearly 1,200,000 tons more than in 1915; the yield per acre (13.93 tons) Is about one and a 'quarter tons more than in 1915, four-fifths of a ton above the aveiage, and the best yield since 1910.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
BELGIAN CHILDREN.

On Christmas Day, in the happy homes of England, where there is good cheer and plenty, we are to be asked to spare somethi,ng to help to make the condition of the children in Belgium a little less pitiable. His Worship the Mayor, in response to the national appeal, has asked the committee representing the Christian Churches, who last year organised a house-to-house collection by means of envel- opes, to take the matter in hand. This they have agreed to do, and the borough will be divided into convenient districts, and 'each Church will be asked ,to be responsible for a defined area. It is to be hoped that all will co-operate so that every household Will have an opportunity of giving. An American medical report, by doctOrs who have recently spent a considerable time in Bel- gium in. connection with the relief work, speaks of the serious condition qt the health of the 'children of Belgium. Tuberculosis and other children's diseases are spreading rapidly, as the result of malnutrition and underfeeding.. Tuberculosis has increased among children over 100 per cent., mainly the effect of insuffi- cient food. There comes to us the cry of the children from despoiled Belgium. Oh, the tragedy of the children of a nation in captivity, especially when the captors, are Germans! All will be glad to respond generously to the appeal which will be made on Christmas Day. We are glad to see the emphatic assurance from the Relief Committee that the food sent out all reaches those for whom it is intended.

MAGNIFICENT ACT OF GENEROSITY.

Orford Hall; the home of the first Mayor of Warrington and Local historian, Mr. William Beamont, and (if several families who in cen- turies long past have figured largely in local public ,life, has, through the generosity of a few public-spirited gentlemen been acquired for, and given to, the town.. The news of the gift was first made .public on Monday • night, when Councillor John Wil- kinson, speaking at a Women's Liberal. gather- ing at the Jelin Crosfield Memorial Hall, made reference to the /natter.'

Since then we have •received a copy or the minutes Of the General Purposes Committee of the Town Council, which contains a. summary of the interesting transaction, and gives credit to the gentlemen to whom the town will for ever he indebted for a notable and noble gift.

Photograph by Birtles,

out the place at the present moment, but they realised the importance of securing it before the whole of the adjacent area was built upon, and are content to leave its full development to the discretion of the Council when normal times return. The only conditions are that the owners or lessees of the adjoining land shall not be asked to pay for paving any new 'streets which may be eonsiderid necessary as boundaries of the property until the same are actually devel- oped for building purposes; that the land shall be used in perpetuity as a Free Public Park and Recreation Ground; and that the Hall and cartilage' shall be preserved and kept inireason. able repair, and applied. as soon as a conveni- ent oppertunity occurs, to some definite public Purpose.

When 1 recently had the pleasure of accom- panying you through the building, I pointed out

ORFORD HALL.

front. with a centre portion set back, and ,was' of the usual pattern of an English moated grange. ,

In .1595 the last of the male Norris died and the estate passed to Elizabeth, the daughter of Thomas Norris, and when, in 1596, she married Thomas Tildesley, the old house became the home of a branch of the Tildesley family and took the name of • the Hall of Tildesley in Orford.

However well pleased the new owner was with the site, he regarded the old building as too modest for his needs and rebuilt it in Jacobean style. It still faced west and the old staircase and other parts of his work may still be found in .the present building. ,Thomas • Tildesley stood well with. his Sovereign and was knighted in 1616.

THE (AFT AND THE GIVERS.

Eight Subscribers Raise The Necessar

t2,Q00.

At the General Purposes Committee the fol- lowing letter from Alderman Bennett was

read:— Market Gate Chambers, Warrington, 150 November, 1916'.•

Dear Mr. Mayor,—l .htve long been anxious that Orford Hall should be acquired by the Corporation, alike on acCount of its historical interest, its inherent picturesqueness, and the urgent necessity for. a Park and Recreation Ground for that end of the borough, which is densely crowded and altogether devoid of amenities of this character.

Some ..months ago I learnt that the property was coming into the market, and ascertained that Ithe owner, Colonel Robert Ireland-Black- burne, 0.13., having regard to his ancestral associations with the place, was prepared to offer it to the town at a low price, and to make a *handsome contribution to the cost. I felt that, in face of the drastic restrictions which have been 4pi)osed upon municipal expenditure, it was not opportune to approach the Council

that it would make a suitable home for perma- nently, disabled soldiers, and I was glad to see, from your speech upon the 9th November, that. yon had taken up the idea. with • your usual promptitude, especially as it has .been in the minds of the subscribers from the first, and is, moreover, capable of immediate attainment. But various other possible uses at once suggest them- selves; for example, a. Military Hospital,-a 'Sol- diers' Club, a Convalescent Home, a Secondary School for Girls, a -School for Defective Chil- dren, or, eventually, perhaps, a Museum, Art Gallery, .Library and Reading Room.-

It is a curious coincidence that the flail as we know it was built in 1716, .exactly two hun- dred years ago; but its history goes back to the thirteenth century, and is closely linked with such well-known local families as the Norrises, the Tildesleys, the llornbys, and the I3eamonts. On that I must not dwell, congenial as the task would prove; but I am Tnfident, Mr. Mayor, that you and all your colleagues on the 63.061 will agree with me that it is peculiarly 'appro- priate that Colonel Blackburne, the descendant of the distinguished botanist who made Orford famous, and whose' family has been connected with the town for generations, should be so' prominently identified with such; a splendid gift. •

I cannot conclude without expressing my warmest personal thanks to him and to the other gentlemen whose generosity p.nd public spirit have enabled me to bring a cherished project to a successful issue. Believe me, faithfully yours, (Signed) Aimirit Br.xxErr. His Worship the Mayor

(Alderman, Peter Peacock), Town Hall, Warrington.

THE THANKS OF THE COUNCIL.:

It was resolved: "That the offer contained in the above letter be gratefully accepted, and that the hearty thanks of the Council be ten- dered to the donors for their generous and bene- ficent gift," and "That the beet thanks of this Council be offered to Alderman Bennett for his untiring _energy and efforts in acquiring Orford Hall and grounds for the town, and that a copy of this resolution, suitably engrossed, be pre- sented to the Alderman." ,These minutes will. be submitted for the approval of the Council on Tuesday evening.

ORFORD HALL.

Its Long And Interesting Story.

By ARTHUR MOUNFIELD.

We peer into a distant past when we try to get our first picture of Orford Hall. It stood upon the present site, a building in wood and

was obtained by purchase by Thomas Black- burne. Curiously enough, he had married, in 1638, Margaret Norris, who belonged to. the family of the original owners of Orford. She came to the home of her ancestors when she Settled there a few years after her marriage. Just two hundred years ago the' building underwent great changes at the hands of Jonathan Blackburne. lie built the south front. which is so familiar to us, and made' this the front of the Hall. The date, 1716; if we remember aright, is over one of the 'win- dows and over the door was placed his family crest, an otter swallowing a fish.' The stairway put in by Thomas Tildesley a century before was left as the back staircase, and a new en- trance hall and stair arranged leading from the new south door. Under Jonathan Blackburne the Hall became famous in more ways than one. People made pilgrimages to see its beauty and naturalists to see the collection of rare plants ana trees by which it was surrounded. An avenue of hone trees, a quarter of a mile in length, led to the entrance, and stood as veteran trunks spoiled by chemicals in 1878, when they were cut down.

A grove of beeches wag planned in .the form of a quincunx and showed straight lines of trees, from whichever point the beholder looked. A beautiful cedar of Lebanon, planted

===== 0002601_19161202_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
as a seedling two centuries ago, stood until destroyed by a hurricane in 1806. Its girth was 13 feet and its branches spread for 60 feet. There was also a Royal Palm, which lived through five generations of the Blackburne family. The greenhouse (a shot-house, to be more correct) was the first in England, and its owner grew within it pineapples (introduced from Holland), tea, coffee, clover, pepper, and sugar-cane. He grew cotton plants, from which one of the family made a cotton dress, in which she arranged to be presented-. at Court; and on the west side of the Hall was an orangery, where oranges and lemons grew. Animals also gave delight to this scientific son of the Blackburnes, and many quaint speci- mens were housed* in , the gardens and out- buildings. Jonatan Blackburn° had a daugh- ter who shared his tastes, and they gave..enter- tainment to many guests of like interests, .who iften made long journeys, to see the collections it Orford.

The hall and grounds were then a rural re- treat of the most beautiful kind. Only the rooks (always the last to leave) and stately architecture remain to remind us of former and vanished glories. The Ireland Blackburnes remained until 1812, when they removed to Hale. For a generation it was the home of the Hornbys, and then came Mr. William Bea.mont, who pursued here the antiquarian studies by which he will always be remembered in Warrington. On or near its two hundredth birthday (the older portion upon its three hundredth birth- day) the building begins a new chapter. May it yet' see its days of greatest beauty; or, if that is too rauoli to hope, may it ente-r upon its days of greatest usefulness,

===== 0002601_19161202_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BEAMOtirS STORY.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
The Changes Of Sewn Centuries.

For the following facts we are largely indebted to the account written by the late Mr. Bearaont in his book "Two Old Lancashire Houses, Hale and Orford.", For fuller details about the Hall we would refer our readers to the work itself in the Warrington Library. Orford Hall, or rather the first house built on the site where the present hall stands, was erected early in the 13th century by one Alan le Norris. At least this family can be located at Orford at this period. We then find that the Lord of • the Manor, William le Boteler, granted to the Norrises and his other tenants around Werington, as it was then called, a charta containing several privileges and rights, and which rights shortly afterwards were incor- porated in the great Magna. Charta.

The Norrises for a time occupied the position of reeve or steward to the Lord of the Manor. As such they were styled in the Boteler -deeds " Senecallus "la Werington. In 1305 Edward I. by royal letter patents appointed William le Boteler and Robert le NoiTays to repair and maintain the bridges of Warrington and Sankey and to levy tolls to pay the expenses thereof. • Mr. Beamont tells us that at this time, the early part of the 13th century, the home or manor place of the family was on the site of the present Orford Hall. It was surrounded by a moat, which admitted no approach except by the drawbridge In the time of Henry VI. (1437) u:e find John Norrys appointed to the oversight of the artil- lery 'which included all arms in the Castles of Chester, Carnarvon, and North Wales. In the Boteler rent rolls we find Thos. le Norreys as a tenant of le mersche,", probably waterlogged land in the - locality of Long- ford. This. name Long-ford, as well as °I.-ford or Overford, suggests the nature of the ground at this time. It is interesting to find from the old records that names which existed five hun- dredyears ago still survive amongst us; for ex- ample, Fairclough, Bold, Houghton, Harrobin, Pierpoint, John Sankey, Garnett. The spelling of both personal and place names varied much. and are very confusing; for example, we have Orford, Overforth, Overford, Orphord and Orfort. •

Coming to the 16th century we find a daughter heir of the Norrises marrying Thos. TiWesley, fl. member of the famous Lancashire .family that took an active part in the Civil Wars, and, it was generally thought, took the wrong side, Mr. Tildesley brought a new name to the Hall, and also gave it a new front; in fact, almost re- built it. The front was made to face the west, and this fact, we think, can still be recognised in the structure. The solid oak staircase with its Jacobean balustres, was then put in, and these may still be seen in what is now the hack stairway.

The strain of what at this period was a heavy financial outlay appears to have impov- erished the family, and Sir Thomas TildeOcY was compelled to realise some of his other pro- perties to meet the cost of the improvements to. Orford Hall..

Unfortunately for themselves, the .Tildesleys. in their many branches, espoused with, devo- tion of the cause of the nouse. of Stuart, and this, from a material standpoint, brought about their ruin.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BLACKBURNES.

In 1638 Thomas Blackburne hooght from Richard Tildesley, on of Sir Thomas TildesleY, Orford Ilan, and came to reside there.

_ • , - The Blaakbtirnes were a branch of n.ancient Yorkshire family. Richard Blackburne built and resided in a fine, old mansion known as Newton. Hall. This Hull is near the present Newton. Railway Station, and can be seen from it in the hollow,ion the south .side. It is ,a picturesque specimen of early inglih archi- tecture of the timber and plaster type. it is 3 pity it cannot be transplanted, as it now. seems much out of Diatetc overshadowed by the railway embankment, and looks a forlorn relic of olden times, .Thomas Blackburne, son of the above, was born at Newton Hall, When ho arrived .at manhocKrs years he .married Mar- garet ,Norris, Who was from a branch of the ancient family or Norris, who for over three centuries resided at Orford Hall. 'Through this lady's marriage the old blood, came back to Orford, and thus we have a Continuity in the family residing at. the Hall extending over six hundred years. - Many references exist showing the ekhs° association of the Blackburnes with the life of Warrington. We read that When Peter Leigh founded. Trinity Chapel he made Jonathan Blackburne , a trustee. In, fact, in all the re- cords . connected with Warrington the lilack- burnes, since they occupied Orford Hall, appear to have played a useful and honourable part. Successive BlackburneS were always the resi- . dent Justices of the Peace, apparently, the only ones in the locality. And as they lived. through troublesome times we may be • sure the office was no sinecure.

Perhaps the most troubled period was when- Prince Charlie invaded England in 1745. It was thought to be the intention of the young Chevalier to march through Preston, Wigan and Warrington to Wales, where it was be- lieved he had many adherents. Orders were given to destroy the bridges, to impede the passage of his troops; and the. bridges at Bar- ton, Frod sham and Warrington were destroyed- The last-named by Colonel graham, with bi'4 Liverpool Blues, who broke down the middle arch. This action 'appears to have diverted the Pretender's Army, who marched to Man- chester instead of through Warrington. 11° doubt to the great relief of the justice residing at Orford Hall.

• We are told that Mr. Blackburne .received the Dike of Cumberland in Warrington and marched With his troops as far, as Wigan in pursuit of the., invaders. Not long after the bells of the. town rang out to celebrate the defeat of, the Stuart Forces at Culloden..

Warrington Bridge was now down; and there was only a ferry, when order was restored and fear of invasion passed. Mr. Blackburne and four others. received a.. Royal Order to 'repair the 'bridge, which was soon restored to its former state.. No doubt the repairing of the bridge suggested to Mr. Blackburne the making of a bridge that would do away with the pas- sage over the brook called Long-ford. This work necessitated laying a 'causeway approach lug the bridge on either side and 'raising tbe embankments to .the brook! The bridge was built, and there it stands "unto this day t° witness. if 1.-lie." .

_ Mention should, be made of 'Mr. Blackburne's love of natural .history. lle planted many' trees, and introduced and successfully grC many trees and. shrubs unknown before in :Eng; south from limes om eln:11 land. Not very many years ago' an avenue stately u*it they did tolfika u e War rintgon smoke, and So they faded and died, and', the ground was swallowed up by the rail'aY and the growing town. These limes were of Mr. Blackburne's planting; also many more of native and imported trees, which in the last century. made Orford flail a place of beautY and interest, and a great resort of naturalists- _Alas! that the impurities of the atniosphere, largely unnecessary as fr;ghtfully swasteful, should have destroyed the efforts of. Mr. Black- burne. It is to be hoped, that under wise borough supervision there will be found those officials who through proper laying out and wise selection of trees and shrubs that will stand 1401 the impure air of Warrington, Orford a. free may again become a place of beauty, park, where .tired toilers and bruised spirits: and perhaps bruised bodice from the war, will find rest and strength through the sweet minis- trations of flat:tire's surroundings.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
NO MAKE-BELIEVE.

Sir Arthur Crosfield On The Peace We Want.

7,000 COMFORTS FOR

SOLDIERS.

-In declaring that we must fight on and not consent to a patched-up peace, Sir Arthur Crostiehl, in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall, on Monday night, reflected the opinion and conviction. of the majority of the nation. The speech was .made during the transition from whist to dancing by about 200 friends, whose ticket .money c,onsiderably augmented the fund of. the Women's Liberal Sewing Class, which has, ever since the beginning of the war, been engaged weekly in making com- forts for soldiers. The social was directly associated with the Orford and Fairfield Wards. • •

Before Sir Arthur spoke, Lady Crosfielil pre- sented the prizes to the winners of the whist and domino games, and, in doing so, she made an . interesting speech. "I have onlY played whist once in my life, and that was at one of these meetings," she said, "and, low and be- hold, at the end of the evening I found I had won the first prize, (Laughter.) That, ,I think, is a consolation for those Who are not 'very good players." She thought that the workers of the Sewing Class were thoroughly entitled to a little enjoyment. Through her time being occupied at the hospital in London, she was not able to visit them as often as she would have wished to, but she was fully informed of the -splendid results obtained from the ladies' meetings, which had been so ably organised by the Committee, the ward'captains, and the indefatigable secretary, Mrs. Osborne. (Ap- plause.) She received a. letter the other day saying that they had already sent over 7,000 comforts to the troops, and, when she spoke to her friends in London, she found that that association could hold its own with any otlicr. (Applause.) Referring to the social that even- ing, her Ladyship thought they ought to be particularly grateful to Mrs. Locker and Mrs. Ralliwell, who were the ward captains respon- sible and had taken so much trouble to make the entertainment a success.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
A LESSON IN CHEERFULNESS.

Sir Arthur first spoke of the pleasure it gave Lady Crosfield and himself to see the people of Warrington looking cheerful in these tragic and sad • times. Every account received from the Front was a lesson to us in cheerfulness. Hav- ing occasion, as he had, to meet people of different nationalities amongst the Allies who were fighting together in this great war, he would say that he always heard the same ex- pressions of, amazement that our soldiers from Lancashire and the whole country not only kept their pecker up, but kept their cheeriness even up to within half an hour, it might be, of taking their lives in their hands to fight for the country and the Empire. (Applause.) He was, therefore, glad to see that Warrington took a leaf out of the, book of the soldiers by keeping cheerful and assembling in- support of a fund which the women turned into garments, not of luxury, but of necessity, for those who faced the cold, wet, mud, and misery at the Froht. All were looking forward to .the time when there would be no need' to make those garments, but, this desire must not result in their acceptance of a make-believe peace, which would only lead to a very sad and dark pros- pect for the men and women Of Europe, and., above all, for those who belonged to the Smaller nationalities. A peace which left the brutal; autocratic, militaiY power of Germany in the saddle and in the ascendancy was not to be thought of. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19161202_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Western Front Climax.

He did not want to be too optimistic, or to attempt any. prophecies—no man in 'England was in a position to prophesy—but, as far as one's information went, he felt they were justi- fied in expecting that in view of the prepara- tions which ' the Allies were making we were likely to see- the war, at any • Fate, on, the Western Front next Spring, if not before, reach something of a climax. He did not say that :that meant the end of the war, .yet, they were justified in hoping that it would bring us with- in sight of the 'closing phases of the war. But we must continue with grim determination, ,for we owed it 'to the men who had died, to the people of 'Belgium, Serbia, Poland, and the 800,000 inhabitants who hat been massacred in Armenia, that a conclusive peace was made— a -peace which would not leave the brutal Prtissian autocracy in the saddle any longer. Applause.) After that, then indeed would. be the time for the :U.S.A. to come in with the Powers of Europe and mike war in the first, instance much legs likely to break out, and, in the long run, a. sheer impossibility. (Applause.) A vote of -thanks to Lady Crosfield was accorded, on the proposition , of Councillor .Locker, seconded by Councillor Halliwell, Special mention also being made of Mr. and Mrs. J. Percival, and Mr. and Mrs. Collins, who were actively engaged in organising weekly whist drives for the sake of the Sewing Class rund.. Councillor Locker mentioned that that association was working solely for the good of the lads, and not,because they wanted to make any political capital out of it. Mr. Percival acted as M.C. The prize-Winners were :—Whist: Ladies-1, Mrs. Coyne; 2, Mrs. Shaw; gentlemen-1, Mrs. Webster (playing as gentleman); 2. Mr. Fleming. Dominoes: 1, Mrs. Dolphin; 2, Mrs. Connor; 3, Mrs. Denton; 4, Mrs- Friar. Mrs. Halliwell, as ward captain, had as Orford Ward helPers: Mesdames Hammersley; Friar, Littlewood, Mellor. Cunnington, and Coyne. In the Fairfield 'Ward Mrs.',Locker had Mesdames Ashton, Woods,' Gibson, Birch and Webster, and Miss Fairhurst.

Mr. G. S. Pendlebury played the piano for the dancing.

ORFORD HALL FOR WAP.RINGTON-

passed into possession of the grandson, again named John Blaekburne, who wth born in. 1754. As a boy he freauently resided with his grand- father at Orford Hall, and received part of his education at the Warrington Grammar School, afterwards at the , university. He was elected as knight of the shire for the county of Lan- caster; and served as Member of Parliament for 46 years, a period covering stirring times in the history of England. He died at Hale in 1833, and was succeeded by his son, John Ireland Blackburne, who was born at Orford Hall, 1783. He, like his father, was educated in his early years at the Warrington Grammar School, the Rev. Edward Owen being master at the time. He *as elected to represent Newton in 1807, and about the same time commanded as Colonel the Warrington local Militia. In - 1835 he again entered Parliament, this time. as a member for Warrington. After a• life full of activity and public service he, like his great grandfather, died at a ripe age in the year 1874, aged 91. He was suCceeded by his son, Colonel John Ireland Blackbiirne, who worthily continued the family traditiodis in rendering public services.

Hale having become the family residence of the Blackburnes, or, rather, the Ireland-Black- 'burnes, Orford Hall was let about 1812 to the Hon. Lucy Hornby, who resided there until her death in 1833. Afterwards it became the home of Mr. Beamont (Warrington's first Mayor) and Mrs. Beamont, of whom Warring- ton will ever feel proud and whom it will long hold in affectionate and honoured remembrance. At the death of Mrs. Beamont, who survived her husband many years, Orford Hall was used for a time as a part of the Warrington Train- ing. College. Lately Orford Hall has been vacant. Through the kindness of Colonel Blackburne• and others it now passes, with 18 •acres of surrounding land, under. the control of the Warrington Borough Council, to be used for some useful public purpose and the grounds for a park for the inhabitants of Warrington for ever,

===== 0002601_19161202_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR NEW MAGISTRATES.

Erish Protest Against Exclusion

From The List.

MR. rVIAGINN'S QUESTIONS.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me space to refer to the recent .appointment of new magistrates for Warrington ?

No one could with justice criticise unfavour- ably the qualifications of the six gentkmen on whom the honour has been conferred. .Every one of them is, in the public estimation, Most worthy; and the selection so far's it *goes is admirable and appropriate. But while the new magistrates are eminently qualified for their office, and are representative of certain sections of the population, they are not sufficiently represen- tative of the whole of the town, and it is to the incompleteness of the list I wish to call public attention. I assume everyone will agree that it is in the best interests' of the town that all sections of the people and every shade of opinion should be associated- with the dispensation of justice in our local Courts, for bic, this means only can universal confidence in the just and impartial administration of the law be estah- lished in the public mind. I think it will also be admitted that when honours and -dis- tinctions are conferred, they should be dis- tributed somewhat' in proportion to the num- bers and the merits of the respective. sec- tions of the inhabitants of the borough. If the present composition of the Warrington Bench is judged by these. standards, I think there' is room for dissatisfac: ion.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
NO REPRESENTATIVES.

For a long period the Irish Nationalists and the Labour parties have had no representa- tive among the magistrates; nor do they appear to be taken into consideration when the appointments are made. The time was when both the parties named had the Satis- faction of being included, and what was then conceded to them should not have been re- tracted afterwards. . I have no doubt, however, that tbe Labour _

element will make their oivniprotest (in fact, they have made it effectually= in Parliamentary action this week),, and I shall confine Myself to a temperate statement of the case for my own people—l mean the Irish residents of Warrington.

As I have already said, ,we have no one to voice our opinions among the. gentlemen who sit in judgment on us. And yet at least one- eighth of the. Population of .Warrington ?re proud to describe themselves as Irish. I know a 'very large proportion of that number are Warrington-born; but still they .desire to retain their national classification.

Is this large fraction of the populace so in- considerable, or° so devoid of capacity, that they are not to 'be taken into account when appointments of new magistrates are being made ? Can it he justly claimed that they ought not • to: have representatives on the Commission of the Peace ? Or can it be argued 'Ulla no share of distinction or honour should pass to them? Are we Irishmen so devoid of civic value or civic virtue that such contemptuous disregard of our feelings can be defended in a democratic age and in an industrial town such. as Warrington ? Or again—have Warrington Irishmen in this frightful war been slack in their response to the call of public duty; have our young men not given as ,good a reply (proportionately to numbers) as any other body of People in the town; and have they nOt displayed 'equal bravery and Upheld with ,equal suCcess The glory of the Army they, have joined % 'These questions, 1 submit, require an answer frimn those authorities with whom it rests to make recomme itiations of local men qualified for the duty of administering the laws. Perhaps it will be said that we Irishmen are so poor in intellect or so bereft of the judicial mind that not one solitary individual can be found capable of creditably discharging the duties of a J.P. If it is—l submit it is a libel. I think we can show amongst us men just as well qualified as the eminently suit- able Liberals and Conservatives 1,0 o• 'have been chosen. I think we have doc' tdia, school- masterS, trade unionists, Friendly , Society workers, merchants and 'business men who could serve the office of magistrate acceptably to the whole town, and with special gratifica- tion to our own section of the population.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
"OLD UNREASONING PREJUDICES."

Everyone is saying what a good thing it is that the old bitterness of party strife is dying out, and predicting that the war will bring into existence a better and more tolerant recognition of .worth; irrespective of political, religious, or. social distinction. The Party truce, however, seems to be a truce only as. between the two great parties; Irish National- ists and the •Labour men seem to be excluded from the benefit of it... I was fondly hoping that the old Unreasoning prejudices against my countrymen were fast disappearing. I thought the war would have at least one good effect—it would show that we are all in our own way and degree useful to the cot/mon cause, and that .no man henceforth these, islands would suffer disability because of his particular religious or political opinions. I thought. also that fair and equitable treatment of .all classes would -remove jealqusY, suspi- Cion and class hatred from the minds of those who heretofore harboured such feelings, and and that no man. 'could say that despite his attainments and high character he was de- barred from positions of henour and emolu- ment unless he consented to wear a particular label.

But incidents such as this appointment of magistrates make one wonder- if the -world is progressing much in tolerance and magnanim- ity after, all. I venture to Swit • that who- ever was responsible for the selection of our new magistrates signally failed to inteilyet the views of, or •do justice ;to the rightful claims of, a large number of Warrington citi- zens; and, what is worse, has given .rise to 'a volume of resentment which will remain until the cause of it is removed.

I therefore.ask you, sir, to allow me to put on record the earnest protest of the Irish people of Warrington against a slight and an injustice.

Thanking you for the use of your space, I am, yours faithfully,

F. :NIAGINN.

Alliance Works, Warrington, November 29th, 191 G.

THIS WEEK'S CASUALTIES.

KILLED.

PERIATE J. WAKEFIELD.

Officer Greatly Impressed By His Spiendid Temperament.

"It is with very great regret- that I have to inform you that your son, • Private J. Wake- field, was killed yesterday (November 13th), about 7.30 p.m.,, by a Machine gun • bullet through the head. death being immediate. Your son's death has cast a grealt gloom over us, as he was much beloved by all his 'comrades. During the short time I have known him I was -greatly impressed .with his quiet, well-behaved

Killed

manner, and his cheerfulness. You have our sincere sympathy in your bereavement." Thus writes an officer, under date November 13th, to Mrs. Wilkefield. „concerning her son, Private James Wakefield, South Lancashire Regiment, who has been killed. His father is engineer at the Fire Station, and resides at 30, Queen-street. Private Wakefield formerly worked at the Cockhedge Factory and had been in France nine months. He was educated at Heathside School and was 21 years of age. His brother, Joseph, was wounded al short time ago'. •

of seven children. A .brother, Ernest Oswald Jones, is serving at Salonica.

Wycliffe PS.A. and Poor Children. There was an 'open meeting -at Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday; presided over by Mr. B. Farrar. The object of the meeting was to hear Mr. Davies, of Manchegter, Plead the cause of Dr. Barriardo's homes for • the poor ° children. The Chairman spoke of Our duty in helping these efforts to save the child life, to give .help, com- fort and peace to the little ones, who are waifs and 'strays. Mr. Davies delivered a•n earnest' address upon the child's life; upon •what they ought to do;. and what the nation-and Govern- ment ought to do. The collection was taken for the two homes for saving the children—those-of Dr. Barnard° and Dr. Stephenson, Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The Chairman gave a hearty welcome •to Sergt. John Oxley, over on ,furlough - from France. He also re- turned thanks for Mr. James Taylor, who is laid on one side With affliction. An interesting meet- ing was closed by singing Rescue the perish- ing."

lir. J. E. Phythian and the Old Disorder.

The last of a series of lectures on Con- stantinople: Its History and Art," was .given by Mr. J. E. Phythtan in ,the 'Co-operative Hall on S aturday evening. When Constnntinople was founded, he said. Rome was only a. little outpost of the Latin peoples, in Ital. London .did not exist for many centuries afterwards; and our own little island was almost unknown, Such ,was the progress of history. Sometimes it would seem that- as if it were not progress, and they felt inclined to say not that the old order had changed to new, but that the old disorder simply came round again, and that if in. the future lite had nothing better for us than to-day, was it worth while 'bringing Children-into the world? But we could hot Convince Ourselvs that this' was a world of unreason, and that there , was' nothing better, and could we not helieve- even in these, dark days that Through the ages one increasing' purpose runs, And the thoughts of • men are widened with the process of ~the suns?

COUNTER DEMONSTRATION

RIFLEM 'Os If . '!HT.

Mr. Trevelyan Interviewed By Second Time Wounded : Lett Leg Aild The Examiner." Foot. Mr. Chas. Trevelyan, M.P., arrived at Bank Quay Station from London yesterday, .but he might as. well not have come, for the .meeting. which he intended to address was not held. But another was. Since Mr. Trevelyan had been announced to speak at the Friends' Meeting House, Butter- market-street, opposition was .abundant through- out the town, and after his trenchant criticism by Mr. Lloyd George in the House of Commons the other evening, it intensified so greatly that. it was feared that if he presented himself there would be ,grave disorder: He stayed away. Throughout yesterday leaflets were distri- buted in profusion, giving the points of Mr. Lloyd George's speech, and asking the people to " come in your thousands" to a protest meet- ing to ..be held ,near the gates of t:114.- Meeting House. Thousands responded to the call. 'When Mr. Trevelyan arrived at Warrington he was informed that representations had been made by the Town Clerk (31. J. Lyon Whittle), acting on behalf of the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock), and as a result the promoters had decided to abandon the meeting. A prominent Friend stated to our representative that the. fact that over 500 bogus tickets had been issued was a great factor in persuading them to cancel the meeting. Mr. Trevelyan could do no other than acquiesce in the decision of the promoters, and he motored to Mr. Ransome's house. to spend a oniet evening. He had to answer a telephone call. It was "The Examiner" man, .askiiig what Mr. Trevelyan thought of it all. He courteously declined to express any opinion on the meeting's abandonment, but was will- ing to dictate the purport of the speech he had intended to deliver.

He now lies in hospital. at Alexandra Park, Stockport. A former employe of 'Messrs. Joseph Crosfleld and .Sons, Ltd.,. Rifleman Smart resided with his wife anti three children at 2, Arthur-street. ===== 0002601_19161202_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
"I HE PROTEST MEETING.

Alcanwhile, the public meeting was being held outside the Friends' Meeting House. It commenced at seven o'clOck and -thousands of people crowded the thoroughfare, making traffic well-nigh impossible. Trams went round the corner of Scotland-road- and -Buttermarket- street even slower than usual. Councillor Henry Roberts, who headed the deputation to .the Mayor on the subject on Thursday, presided over the meeting, The crowd was well-behaved and very orderly.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE AT THE RIGHT TIME.

The Chairman said he had just been informed by their respected Chief Constable that these foolish and infatuated people who thought they could come to Warrington to talk about peace at a time like this had made discretion the better part of .valour and decided to abandon the meeting. (Cheers.) They might, therefore, take it that no meeting would be held 'that night .in favour of peace. "We are quite pre- pared," went on the speaker, to make peace when we are in a position to make the terms of. peace." These people, who had done nothing for the success of our arm*, should mind their own business and leave those who .paid the price to settle the account. (Cheers.) He had always been proud of his town and the com- mon. sense of his people, and he was more than proud of the loyalty., and Patriotism which the men and women of Warrington had shown in this, great crisis.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
A WAR TO THE DEATH.

Councillor John Brotherton (,Salfor ' said that "Murphy had not sung to-night," at any rate, if he had done so, he had done it from the sa(e seclusion of the .warm filleside. He (the speaker) was 'sorry to be informed that there. were such people in Warrington, seine. of them who were waxing fat in the manufac- ture of munitions of If ar and prating about peace. Had he come they would have seen to it in the most peaceful fashion ,that he did , not speak in that building. " They Want peace," said the speaker, "so *do we, but, ladies. and gentlemen, we are going to get it, and not in the way that Mr. Trevelyan, Mr. .Morell, Ramsay Macdonald, Philip Snowden, and Mr. Pringle, and men of that description, would get peace. We are going to get peace on our own dictation and pa our own tern's, and not theirs." (Cheers.) Mr. Trevelyan, he went on, would say that we ire only fighting the Kaiser and the top* nobs; but we h,re at war with their nation; we are at war with' the• lot; and this war is not going. to end until We have exterminated the lot and crushed into the gutter Kaiserism and Prussian militarism, to such an 'extent that it 'can never again rear its head. When this is done we will have peace, but for tlio.inoment it is war to the death, .and war upon all. Germans. (Cheers.) They in Warrington had taken care that no blatant demagol.cue enjoying the fruits and benefits of being a ,Member. of Parliament and (halving 400 a yeaif, whilst he was repudiated. by his own peoMe,; who had called upon him to re- sign his'seat, would address them. He thought that it would k.)e perfectly safe to come to that sleepy old town of Warrington to gull people by his peace prattle. They were there to shdw ,carpet-baggers of the Trevelyan type that the .people of Warrington had made up their minds that this thing, whatever it might cost, even if it costs them their last man and their last shilling; would be seen through, without either the help or hinderance of the Trevelyan type. ((: Peers.) "PEACE PROGRAMME CONDEMNED." Cmtneillor. James Broadhurst proposed the following resolution:— " This meeting condemns the enunciation of a peace programme by Mr. Trevelyan and those ' associated, with him, inasmuch as they have obstructed every effort made by the Government for the successful prosecution. of the war. " This meeting further considers that Mr. Tre.velyan's visit to Warrington is an insult to the loyalty and patriotism of the town. "The satred memory of those whose lives have been freely given for England in. this just war, and the sick and wounded men in our midst, demand that only. those who have borne the anxiety and loss and made the sacrifice which this war has entailed shall settle the terms of peace which are necessary to secure for ever the safety of 'the Allied peoples?'

===== 0002601_19161202_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER KIND OF CONSCIENCE.

Councillor Broadhui-t added that he, as well as so-called conscientious objectors, was willing to sutler for conscience' sake, and would will- ingly have gone to the ,Police court if it had been necessary, as he did not intend that Mr. Trevelyan should speak. Councillor Dale spoke in favour of the 'resolu- tion, and mentioned that he had brought along with him a Cousin; il,vho was a Camulian sol- dier. (Cheers.) The .people who should make peace were those who were helping to win, the war.

Councillor Wilson ,expressed the opinion that everyone who was against England should be shunted out of the country. To Think that religious .building should be defiled as proposed that night was .absolutely scandalous. The Councillor threatened that if ever a peace Meet- ing were held in that place again he and his friends would storm it.

The resolution was carried with great enthu- siasm and waving of hats. A resolution of thanks was passed to Councillor Roberts amid chrees.

The National Anthem was sung, three cheers were raised for the war, and three boohs were given for Mr. Trevelyan.

ST. MARY'S HALL.

Lady Crosfield Opens Attractive

Sale Of Work.

A GOOD BEGINNING.

• A Sale of work, opened auspiciously on Thursday awl continued yesterday and to-day, bade .fair to meet the debt due on St. Mary's Hall, a well-equipped building erected to meet the requirements of Roman Catholic residents in Warrington. The opening ceremony on Thursday was gracefully performed by Lady Crosfield, who was 'presented with a beautiful bouquet by Miss Helena Marion Broady. The Rev. A. V. Wilson, 0.5.8., presided, and those present included the Rev. T. A. Hind, rector of St. Mary's; and the Very Rev. J. P. Whittle, 0.5:13., the Rev. 11. B. ilrimavesi,

0.5.8., St. Ann's, Liverpool' ; Di. Wilson, St. Peter's, Liverpool; Dr. Mercer, 0.5.8., lostock Hall; Father Darby; Parbold ; Fathers Jackson and Rylance, 0.5.8., St. Benedict's ; Fathers Dawson and Chamberlain, 0.5.8., St. Alban' ; Father Murphy, Sacred, Heart; Fathers J. P. Willson, B. Hayes, 0.5.8., St. Mary's; Mrs. Roberts, and the presidents of the various stalls.

LADY CROSFIELD AND CIVILISATION. The Reverend Chairman said Lady Crosfield needed no introduction to any section of the people of Warrington. She had made herself well known and endeared herself to them all by the kindly interest she had shown in Warring 2 ton's various enterprises and the welfare of the people. Her 'benevolent work at Highgate on behalf of, wounded soldiers was greatly appre- ciated. (Applause.) She had also charmed them with her accomplishments. ' Lady Crosfield; who Was Warmly received, com- plimented those present on the completion of such a beautiful hall. It was well worth all the efforts they might make- or had been mak- ing, on its behalf. She was amazed at the beauty of the hall, and the sale of work so ably prepared by the stallholders. As the Ger- mans had made clear their • determination to bring down the whole superstructure of English civilisation, it should be made clear by self- sacrifice and continuing our work that there were limits to the destruction which we would permit them to accomplish. We ,had also to send comforts to our soldiers, who were fighting 'so cheerfully for us at the Front, and they §t. Mary's parish had shown that they could do both. (Applause.) She had much pleasure in declaring the sale of work open. (Applause.) The Rev. T. A. Hind, in moving a vote of thanks to Lady Crosfield for the very generous act she • had performed, also took occasion to express the gratitude of the people of St. Mary's, first of all to Mrs. W. B. Roberts for her kindness in bringing her well-known orchestra, and the townspeople of Warrington; • who had Wien exceedingly kind. To Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons they owed a debt of gratitude. They had put up the stalls and decorations, and Messrs. Greepall had given a very generous subscription. - The officials were :—President, Rev. T. A. Hind, 0.5.8.; treasurer, Mr. W. Dilworth, secretary, Mr. A. Kilburn; bank treasurer, Mr. James Magrath ; stewards, Messrs. J. Eekers- ley, 11. Houghton. J. Evans, W. Challoner, T. Ridgway, A. Beswick, Duffy, Gordon, Johnson and Taylor.

FRIDAYS OPENING.

First Day's Proceeds Amount To £223.

The opening ceremony yesterday was per- formed by his Worship the Mayor, who, with the Mayoress (Mrs: Peacock), attended the sale of work. •

The Rev. R. B. Primavesi, who presided, said that the Mayor had acted most impartially for the benefit of the town, and he had been kind on every occasion to Catholics when they asked him to dO anything for them. (Applause.) The Chairnran added- that the takings on Thursday 'were 11223. The Mayor, in declaring the sal ek open, said he believed he was one of the founders of that -Hall.:

Miss Bernardine Kilburn presented a bouquet to the Mayoress and Master Wm. henry Lea a buttonhole to the Mayor.

The Rev. A. V. Wilson pro Posed a vote of thanks to the Mayor. The Rev. T. A. Hind, seconding, said that while Liverpool had a Lord Mayor and Lady Mayoress, Warrington had a Mayor who was a lord and a Mayoress who was a lady. (Ap- plause) Whenever his Worship required the 'hall for any war charity or public charity the people of St. Mary's would always be glad to let him have the use Of it, and he could say that it had the finest dancing\tloor in Warring- ton.

The 3ltiyor, in reply, said 'the Roman Catho- lics had been amongst his greatest and most loyal friends, and he had a very warm heart for the Catholic section of Warrington. The sale will be continued to-day, the open- ing ceremony being performed by the Rev. J. P. Whittle.

II I. It I . H. OGDEN AM) FREE SPEECH.

Arising out of the refusal of a hearing to Mr. Trevelyan, the Rev. H. Ogden intends to preach, in Golborne-street Baptist Church, on Sunday evening, upon "The Right of Free Speech." It is almost unnecessary to add that the reverend gentleman has been an advocate ,for the prosecution of the war since, its incep- tion, and it is upon the fundamental principle of the freedom of speech that he intends to protest.
