===== 0002601_19161202_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Caledonians At The

Festive Board.

SPIRIT OF NATIONALITY.

The twenty-seventh annual dinner of the Warrington Caledonian Association was held at the Lion Hotel on Thursday, when Mr. W. Murray presided. There was a very large gathering, including many visitors, the princi-

pal guests being his Worship the Mayor (Alder- man Peacock) and Mr. J. Upson, who proposed the toast of the evening in glowing terms. There was a long toast list and quite a number of happy speeches were made in the course of a most enjoyable evening. . The menu included, of course, the Haggis, that king of dishes, heralded by a skin of the, pipes and escorted twice round the banqueting hall. St. Andrew's pudding concluded an attractive dinner list. •

Musical items were interspersed with the speeches, and amongst those- who kindly sang were Mrs. Sanders, Miss Fleming, Captain Stott, Captain Mathaws, and Mr. A. W. Pearce. W. H. Payton accompanied. r The loyal toasts having been duly honoured, I Mr. J. L. Davenport proposed "His Majesty's .Forces." The Navy was ready, aye, ready. As an island nation we depended upon our Over- seas possessions for our food supplies and the •material for our many industries. Our Navy to-day has maintained its supremacy on the high seas not only for our defence, but for the defence of every civilised -nation in the earth.. (Hear, hear.) Referring to the Army, the speaker mentioned the magnificent training which had brought our new -.armies to such a state of efficiency as had been displayed on ninny battlefields in France and other parts of the world. We had been called a nation of shopkeepers, hut it would be interesting to get an idea of what the Prussian Guard thought of the qualities of the shopkeepers they .met in France.. (Hear, hear.)

Captain Heaton, in reply. said it had been a \ revelation to him how everyone in Warrington thought of the boys at the Front, and the boys out there had a strong feeling for home.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
"SCOTLAND FOR EVER."

The Chairman read the following telegram received from Mr. James Munro, patron of the society :—" Edinburgh, Friends around the table set, blytlie am I to know you're met: all as cantie as you can and all your ills forgot. Sorry cannot join you. Scotland for ever." (Applause)" Mr. John Upson, who proposed the toast of "Scotland," said his subject was a very large one, and he might very properly deal with the central position Scotland occupies in the lime- light of history—the' ilory of its literature, I and especially the beauty and attractiveness of its ballad poetry. One might dwell upon the ,glorious .Military record of the Scottish race: the foremost place it occupies in industry and !Commerce; in modern science and mental 'philosophy. His own bias was towards the educational system of Scotland. He had often asked himself why Scotsmen, in spite of the numerical smallness of the people. loom so largely in the eyes of ,the world, and he thought this pre-eminence had not been got by superior intellect or ability, but to the .fact that the spirit Of nationality had never been lost. The courage that had never faltered in the face of defeat had made Scotsmen the pioneers in the struggle for liberty and for freedom. To-day the Scot is as heroic as his fathers were and he believed the Scot would ever be found in the van ot rogress—the champion of liberty, who will keep for ages the flag unsullied and the"

lustre undimmed of the great principles of nationality and-humanity that had been placed in his hands. lie aoked them to drink to Scotland', 'O4 'hind of high...achievement, the birthplace of liberty, the land Of heroic sone. immortal in history, the land of poetry and liberty. (Applause.) -

Thewas enthusiastically honoured.

WHY SCOTSMEN SUCCEED.

Captain Mathams, who responded, compli- mented gr. Upson , upon the speech he had made, and said that it was because the spirit of Scotland was in their souls that they suc- ceeded. Scotland was a little, country, moun- tainous—barren, if they liked—and every Scotsman was horn amidst the 'warring of the elements—his life was a struggle against adver- sity—and the concentrated struggles of genera- tions of Scotsmen had helped him by education an dgood living to high ideals. Born into an atmosphere of adversity, he staits out the in- heritor of all the 'privileges and all the tradi- tions Of a great Empire. They felt that Scotland was their home and that they ought to be worthy of it.

Mr. J. 'McDermid proposed " The Town we live in," coupled with the name of his Worship the Mayor. He referred to the splendid part. Warrington had taken in the present national crisis. Owing to the diverse character and importance of her industries she had been called upon to discharge a very high duty—not only to contribute to the fighting forces of the Crow 4 but to supply the means by which they night attain the objects for which they set out. She had sent valuable contributions of her sons to every branch of the Forces, on sea or land, overhead, or under, and the town whieh had contributed to the fighting forces a battalion or regiment like the 4th South Lancashires, which had earned for itself the name of "The :Unbreakable Fourth," had done well for the common cause. (Applause.) Speaking of the Corporation and trade of War- rington, he said that never as long as. he had been associated with it had the Corporation been so worthy of the town as at the. present Their experience' of the last year or ,wo, without munichial contests, was that with the able guidance of their worthy Mayor, wllo displayed a firmness, tact and sagacity nevet- equalled, the Corporation had bee 4 ,a pride to the town. (Hear, hear.) The Mayor had suc- ceeded in everything he undertook and had displayed an enterprise, energy and enthusiasm which made' him a pattern to every citizen in the town. (Applause.)

iine.

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By cßtric.

The Tribunal still keeps pretty busy.

Who was responsible for rousing one of our military representatives into a state of fury at the last meeting?

I noticed .the caretaker of ,aur Council Cham- bers cleaning the largo brass plate by the door, which informs .casual visitors what this promi- nent building really is.

Perhaps it is intended that the shortest sighted ratepayer shall not mistake the place when he comes to pay his dues.

I notice that the poor rate notices hare just been sent out. _

Revelry reigned supreme at the Brookfield Hospital last Wednesday night.

The wounded soldiers entertained their staff with a "ripping"' concert.

"The Examiner" has earmarked £2l from the fnnd it is ettilecting for Christmas Puddings for the Cheshires. Lymm has contributed some portion of that .01. The time is now running short for those who intend to help locally.

Pun ran fast and furious at tlie Heatley Bowling supper last Thursday. -

About seventy-five members and friends par- took of a hearty repast, ably provided by tie hostess, Mrs. Richardson, and her splendid staff.

May I take this opportunity of icompliment- jag Mr. A. E. Davies on his splendid perform- ance in the "Coronation Cup" handicap.

Better luck next time.

What Sergeant J. H. Moston Thinks.--Serg,t. J. H. Moston, writing to his brother at Booth's Hill, says:—" I think we shall be on this job a long time yet, but I don't mind if they will keep us here for the winter. We are at a grand place and can get almost anything we want, although Nrr are quite near to the trenches. Things are moderately quiet."

Present at Urban Council.—Councillor F. Lockyer presided at the monthly meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council on Monday.. There were also present: Councillors W. G. Groves, S. Critchley,. C. E. Cowper. It. H. Gotobed; and A. Mclntyre; Mr. W. MuHard (clerk), Dr. Burrowes (acting medical officer of health), Mr. D. Mort (surveyor), Mr. C. I. Barnes (water manager), and Mr. T. 11. West (gas manager).

Read Improvement.-7-At a meeting of the

Roads Committee the Surveyor produCed a plan of the suggested alteration of the road in Lymm—Hey-lane and under the railway. It was resolved that the tracing be sent to the London and North-Western Railway Company and that they be asked whether they would contribute to the cost.—The action of the Roads Committee was confirmed by the Council on Monday evening.

Primitive Methodist Church Anniversary.— The Primitive Methodist Church anniversary was held last Sunday. Both services were con- ducted by Mr. Stamp, a student of the Hartley College, Manchester, and were 'well attended. Special hymns were sung and the offertories were in aid of the church funds. On Monday evening a public tea and lecture were held in the adjoining schoolroom, and about a hundred members and friends sat down to as hearty repast. The lecture, entitled "The Religious Faith of Modern Statesmen," was ably deliv- ered by the Rev. James Lockhart, who, it is interesting to know, will, in the year 1918, be the Principal of the Hartley- College. The entire proceeds amounted to a little over £3l.

AUGMENTING NATION'S FOOD.

Useful Proposition Adopted By The

Urban Council.

The Urban District Council, on Monday evening, decided to do their bit towards in- creasing the food supply.

Councillor Gotobed moved that in the case, of allotments let by the Council between the present time and the 25th March the rent shall commence on the latter date, and that allotment tenants. and those who may become so, shall be allowed ,to prepare the land in the mean- while. He hoped they would have the whole of the field taken up. They saw signs of the scarcity and high prices of food, and this con- cession should he an inducement to everyone who had the time to do something to augment the nation's food supply at such a critical time. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Cowper seconded the motion, which was passed.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE AND LIGHTS.

hostile Criticism At The Council Meeting.

When the business of the Lymm Urban IME- trict Council had finished on Monday night, Councillor Cowper referred to the prosecution of shopkeepers in the village for breaches of the lighting regulations. Why didn't this police- man go to some big houses? The way tyranny w.as being held over the village was prepos- terous. The police ought to know they were the servants of the public and not -their masters. If these people got up a petition he would sign his name at the top of it. Councillor Gotobed said that while he did not agree by a long way with what Councillor CW'per had said, he did feel that they were having a little too much attention in the village. He agreed with Councillor Cowper that the police might spend a little more time outside the village than upon those who were trying to get their living in shops. Councillor Cowper suggested that the Gas Manager should have the sides of the kerb- stones whitened, as was being done in Man- chester.

GREEN DRAGON BOWLING CLUB.

Annual Dinner And Presentation 01

Prizes.

The annual dinner and presentation of prizes for the Green Dragon Bowling Club, Heaney, took •place. ,at, the Green Dragon Hotel, on Thursday evening, and was enjoyed by about

seventy members and friends of the club. Thanks to Mrs. Richardson (the hostess) appe- tites were soon satisfied, and everyone voted the dinner, as in former years. a splendid suc- cess.

After dinner the usual "smoker" was held. Some very fine singing was heard, and, on the whole, a very enjoyable evening was spent. The chair was taken by the President (Mr. J. Bolton), who also presented the successful members with their prizes. The second prize of the Coronation Cup was won by Mr. A. E. Davies, a popular member of the Heatley Club. This cup is, perhaps, the "blue ribbon" of the Lancashire and. Cheshire greens, and is-,:giien under the auspices of the British •Crown Green Amateur Bowling Association. Np fewer thln 560 members competed for this coveted trophy, and out of that great number the Heatley man was successful in getting into the final round. The final was played on the Roebuck Hotel Green, Flixton, Mr. Davies' opponent being Mr. Gleave, jun., the well-known Warrington ,bowler. The second prize, which was presented Ito Mr. Davies on Thursday night, is a hand- some case of cutlery. comprising 53 pieces. , Other prizes and prize-winners were:— First Club Handicap.-,-1, Mr. J. Other special prizes were won by Mr. W. Jack- son, Mr. H. Bell, Mr. W. Daniels, Mr. H. Bell, Mr. T. Bell. Mr. H. Leigh, Mr. W. Daniels, Mr. S. Bell, Mr. E. A. Flemming, and Mr. T. Wilson.

THE 'EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1916.

CAPTAIN G. V% . C.

Popular Local Officer Home On Leave.

Captain G. W. C. Hartley, Cheskire Regi- ment, one of our most popular local officers, is home on leave from France. He arrived in England last Saturday night and stays until IMonday evening, when he returns again to France. Captain Hartley, it will be remem- bered, was in charge of our local men when they were mobilised on that eventful fourth of August, and has remained with them _since, lexcepting for a few weeks in which he has been attached to headquarters and officiating las town major-over a prominent French town. Talking to our representative ,at the Volunteer Headquarters, in the early hours of Tuesday morning, he assured him that the Lymm lads were as happy as one could expect.. "They are a good lot of men," he said, "and I 'am naturally proud of them." Asked how he liked his change; he said it was a nice change from the trenches, but he was looking forward- to going back to the men at an early date.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Proud of a Decorated " Lyrnm Lad."

"About twelve months ago," he said, "I had cause to recommend a Lymm lad for-very good work, and as nothing cazne 'of it I thought it had fallen through, but the other day, when I was actinc, as aide-de-camp to a distinguished officer, I had the pleasure of seeing him decor- ated with the Military Medal. It was my duty to hand the officer the ribbons, and you can quite imagine how proud I felt when I handed him the ribbon for him to decorate a Lymm lad--Quartermaster-Sergt. Jack Fisher. It was an honour well earned; fdr'he' is a good man and a credit to his company." Captain Hartley is/looking the picture of health, and looks as well as, if not 1 shade better than when he commanded our local Territorials in peace time. He has been at the Front since February, 1915, and up to the present has been successful enough to come through mahout a scratch. Its is a splendid fellow and a model officer. Ever ready to study the comfort of his men, he won the admiration of them all.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
Q.M.S. JACK FISHER.

Awarded The Military Medal Within Hearing 01 The Guns.

A SPLENDID CAREER.

IQuartermaster Sergeant Jack Fisher, well known in Lymm and High Legh, and a member of the Cheshire Regiment, has been awarded the ,Military Medal for good work performed about twelve months ago. This good news was brought to light during an interview by our representative with Captain G. W. C. Hartley, his company commander, now home on a few days' leave. This pleasant ceremony was per- formed a few days ago "somewhere in France," within easy hearing of the boom of the guns. He was recommended for recognition by Capt. Hartley about twelve months ago for good work performed While under fire, and, by a happy coincidence, on the day on which the decoration was conferred Captain Hartley was acting aide-de-camp to the distinguished officer whose duty it was to pin on the decorations. I Q.M.-Sergt. Fisher is a typical soldier, and, to use a comrade's term, is "a soldier to his !finger tips." He joined the Cheshire Regiment 'about two years previous to the war commenc- ing and during his first year's service he was successful in winning the recruits' prize offered . .

to the best recruit of the year. During his second year's service he was promoted to the rank of Lance-Corporal. Then came the war, and during his fine month's training he was promoted to full Corporal. In the course of about another three months, while training in Suffolk, he obtained his third degree and was made a full Sergeant. He was drafted out to France in February, 1915, and after several months' trench work he was made Quarter- master-Sergeant over the company to which the Lymm lads belong.

Quartermaster-Sergeant Fisher, previous to the war, resided with his parents at High Legh. He was an apprentice plumber, em- ployed by Colonel Legh, on the High Legh Estate.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TRIBUNAL.

Chairman, Mr. Samuel Gould

Representatives: Military, Messrs: A. de C. Parmiter and F. E. Evans; Board of Agricul- ture, Mr. Newton Worthington. Publican Who Helps Farmers.

A. licensee (36), married, who asked Ton con- ditional exemption, stated., that when..he was before them last he was granted a period of ex- emption conditionally on his helping the farmers in the district. Since that time he had helped various farmers a matter of four or five days a week, and was 'quite willing to carry on with this work of national importance. It would lie more satisfactory if he were allowed to take work with one farmer as a regular hand. He had a large sum of money invested in his business.—Do you mean to say that you could give all your time to farming? Except Saturday,, sir.—How many farmers have you assisted?, Four.—Applicant, who is classified in 81,- was granted temporary exemption nntil March Ist, subject to assisting the farmers and joining the V.T.C., the Chairman remarking that there was nothing to hinder_ him obtaining a regular situation.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
Farmer for his Hay Cutter.

A farmer who appealed for a man_ (36), mar- ried, stated that he was his hay cutter and assisted him generally on the farm. He had been in his employ for a period of 10 years, and he was the only hay cutter he had left out of three before the wan—Temporary exemption till March- Ist, ,subject to rensaining in present employment.

Doing Tileir Bit.

Mr. Baker, solicitor, of A ltrinchare,. asked for the conditional exemption of two farmers, part- ners in a large farm, and a man who was their teamsman. They were doing more for their country by remaining in. their present occupa- tion than they would be if they were in the Army. Their farm was 170 acres, 150 of which were arable; andfrthey.had,eight working horses and 40 head of cattle. Besides, one-of them was the sole tenant of a farm of 35 acres, most of which was arable. The teamsmau was abso- lutely indispensable, and had been accustomed to farm work all his life.—The Chairman re- marked that the food question was going to be a .serious Matter, and this particular case was one of the best they had had before theth. It was quite clear that the meg, were doing their best by getting as much out of the farm as pos- sible, and the Tribunal decided to grant condi- tional exemption to the BI and C 2 men, and temporary :exemption to the Ist March to the Al man, subject to remaining in present occupa- tion.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HE ATH.

Mr. W. Mclntyre on Military Tribunal.—At Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council, Mr. William Mclntyre, of Stock- ton Heath, was elected as a member of the District Military Tribunal, in the room of Mr. Edwin Britt+. deceased.

PENKETH.

Accident to a Boy.—On Sunday morning Eric Hotigh, aged five years, returning from Sunday school to his home in Stocks-lane, was acciden- tally knocked down by a motor cyclist passing through the village. As far as can be gathered the lad was walking from one side of the road to the other, and either did not hear or heed the motor horn behind him. A rather serious cut on the back of the head and a severe shak- ing were the result. The cyclist, after haying him '•medically attended, cs?,rried .him ,home, where he is progressing favourably.

. Recommended for Military Mees;.l.—Sapper Thomas Rigby, Royal Engineers, writing home to his parents at South-lane„ under the date of Saturday, November 24th, says that he is in the best of health and on his, way back from the trenches for a week or two to carry out some important work for which he is detailed. The most interesting item runs as follows: "I might have some good news for you in a week or two, as I have been recommended for the Military Medal. Of (course, I hope that I shall get it, especially as it will be the first award our way. I had some very hard an dangerous work to accomplish, whichiq was fortunate enough to complete, so the officer in charge forwarded my name to headquarters with .a strong recom- mend, telling me at the same time that he hoped I should have the honour of receiving it. lam hoping to get home on leave early in the New Year, so in the meantime do not worry' if you don't receive many letters."

MISSIONARY WORK.

Natives Prefer British To German Rule.

On Sunday the annual missionary services were preached in the -Wesleyan Chapel by the Rev. F. Hudson, resident minister, late mission- ary in the Transvaal. The following evening a special missionary meeting was held in the schoolroom, the Speakers being, the Rev. F. Hud- son and the Rev. W. T. Derry, the chair being taken by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw.. A very favourable report of ,the work accomplished by the Warrington branch of the Wesleyan Metho- dist Missionary Society was read by the Rev. J. Gunnell. The Rev. W. T. Derry was emp;nrie

over the special need for more strenuous eo-ts in missionary work in Africa, as Mohammedism is rapidly gaining ground there. The Rev. F. Hudson gave an interesting outline of his work in Bechuanaland and the Transvaal. As a proof of the good work of the missionaries done for the Empire, apart from its religious aspect, was the fact that long before the war hundreds of natives had trekked hundreds of miles from German West Africa and formed settlements under British rule. Where the natives have become Christianised they have begged the Brit- ish Government to tax them for the express purpose of educating the young people. The result is some fine permanent schools and a high standard of education. The collections for both days amounted to .C 22 us. 4d.

Tennis and Bowling Club.—The members of the /Bowling Club spent a very enjoyable even- ing on Wednesday, when a hotpot supper was provided in the pavilion. A very interesting game of cards, usually known as "Slippery Sam," was played, the prize-winners being 'Mr. P. H. Bennett and Mr. Warburton.

PRIVATE .1. CRITCHLEY.

Shell Bursts Two And A Half Feet

Away..

. Private James Critchley, Worcestershire Regiment, has been wounded in action and lies in hospital in France. Writing to his home, Boundary Farm, Padgate, he states :—" I may tell you that the wound is only slight. A shell burst about two and a half feet away from me, on my left-hand side, wounding me in the forehead. I lost all my kit except a spoon, and walked out of ,the. trenches with several others who had been hit. . . ."

A letter received on Thursday said Critchley was suilering from shock and was now in Scot- land.

An "old boy" of Padgate C.E. School, Private Critchley enlisted if' May of this year, proceeding to France on September 14th. He worked on his father's farm, and has .a brother —Richard--in the Machine Gun Corps. He attended Padgate Church and is 27 years of age.

-**-. - EXTRAORDINARY RACES.

We are all familiar with the humorous races that diversify the 'serious part of the programme at meetings for the determina- tion of athletic contests. We have all seen men diving through barrels, 'plunging .under tarpaulins,2 catchng apples at the ends of strings with t/aeir mouths, and performing othek diverting and comical antics for the benefit of the crowd. Probably to the greater part of the spectators these afford more amusement than the more serious and digni- fied races a-foot or, a-wheel. A passion for racing either yourself or anybody or any- thing else is world-wide and of great anti- quity. Some of the most remarkab..e contests have been the rejmits of wagers made in un- reflecting moments. In the reign of Queen Elizabeth Sir John Pakington, who was known to the courtiers as "Lusty Pakington," and to Queen Eliza- beth as "My Temperance," laid a,wager of three thousand plunds to swim against three noble courtiers from Westminster Bridge to Greenwich, but the Queen interposed and forbade the contest, so that the spectacle of four nob:es engaged in a swimming contest in the Thames has not yet taken place. - In 1809 Captain Barclay undertook to walk a thousand miles in a thousand hours for a sum of three thousand pounds. When he ar- rived. at Newmarket at the conclusion of his undertaking the bel:s rang out a merry peal of ,greeting, and he got almost as rousing a we.come as a popular hero.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
RACES OF RIDERLESS HORSES.

It was the custom at one time to hold races of riderless horses down the long straight. Corso at Rome, at the time of the Carnival. The horses were decked with spur, sheets of tin and other objects, the purpose of which was so to frighten and excite the animals that they would be urged to their utmost speed without the assistance of any jockeys. The horses were arranged in a line behind a rope. After a few seconds the rope was removed, and the hones flew way at a furious pace down the Corso. As the course wa,s often crowded with unprotected pedes- trians, considerable damage was done by the plunging, kicking steeds. In the Far East, animal races are generally a feature of the gymkhana organised by the English. Any anima: may be entered for such a contest, a hare, a cpw, an alligator, a frog or a snake. The handicappers give each animal what the-- consider a fair amount of start, bearing in mind the respective speeds and intelli- gences of the competitors. The owner of the beast is allowed to follow the wearer. of his, colours, and guide him with a stick but he may not drive or urge him in any way what- vver. Most unexpected results ensue; a cow or a dog will bolt into the crowd; the ele- phant will turn round and forge furiously ahead in the wrong direction, while a great fat waddling swan will toddle with offended dignity and much protestation straight to the winning post. The race in most cases is decidedly not to the swift. _ .

Paris -provides two forms of eccentric sport in snail racing and,eask rolling. The snail is not exactly the creature one would at first sight choose for exhibitions of speed. But foreign military men in particular have been known to be addicted to this so-called sport, and the value of a specially speedy mollusc has been stated to be as much as one hundred pounds. Of course, it is only as a means of gamsbang that even the fleetest snail that ever crawled can possesl, such a stupidly fic- titious'value. °tisk rolling is engaged in by the employees of the cooperages in the vicin• ity of the French capital, and provides a fair amount of amusement.

A MILE OF MACARONI. Two Italian igentlemen, possessing con- siderable powers n the way of rapid mastica- tion and, effective digestion once challenged each other to a contest to see who could most qiiickly swallow a mile of macaroni. The winner, a certain Signor Beraccio won by 130 yards. His subsequent fate is unknown to me.

Folly is not confined to any nationality or age. And we could find plenty of instances of stupid contests within the borders of the British Isles. On one occasion two men chal- lenged each other to run from a given vil- lage into Leicester, bare footed through the snow. The winner arrived eighteen minutes before his opponent, but his sense of joy was diminished when he discovered that his feet were so frost-bitten that he had. to lose sev-

eral of his toes. His opponent also shared the same fate, but he, poor fellow, had no joy o victory, with which to solace •him.self. more perilous race was commenced as the result of a wager made in a fit of drunken- ness. A man who possessed two powerful bloodhounds was boasting of their wonderful abi:ity in rapidly following up a scent. One of his auditors challenged the statement and undertook to reach an in, some five miles distant if he were given a. start of ten min- utes. The dogs were let loose upon the track of the fleeing man ere the ten minutes had elapsed, and he managed to reach the door of the inn on:y just in time to slam the door in the teeth of his pursuers.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
A. SHAVING COMPETITION.

I have often wondered what were the feel- ings of the men who submitted themselves to be shaved by two barbers in the West End of London, in the determination of the rela- tive abilities of the two gentlemen with the razors. It is true they got a shave for no- thing, but they also stood the risk of getting a few cuts about the face at the same time. However, such was* the skill of the contest- ants, that no one was hurt, although the vic- tor shaved eighty-two sitters within an hour. In a way, the long distance Marathon races with which wo are now so familiar are quite extraordinary. Most people believe that the competitors are emulating the feat of a fam- ous Greek athlete who ran from Marathon to Athens with the news of a great Greek Vic- tory, and fell down dead, as he delivered the joyful tidings. And People who object to these isplays; on, other grounds extend to theM their ,patronage and encouragement* under the idea that they are perpetuating in this, way the memory of a singularly patriot- ic deed. Now, as a matter of fact, this is a pure legend, for which there is no historical evidence whatever, and what is more, in the Olympic games that were held for so many years at Athens there was no such race as the Marathon race, either as a memorial of a cral:ant deed or as a test of endurance. The 0 present Marathon race is a pure invention of recent organisers, and has no basis either in ancient ' history or less ancient but still ancient practice.

STRANGE CONTESTS IN SIAM. Siam affords a few instances of strange contests. Of these we may notice first the boat races. The object of the competitors is, as is usual on these occasions, to reach a cer- tain mark first. But the rules of the game as played in Siam permit of the fouling of your opponent's boat. Hence your first object is not to go faster than your opponent but to reach' him as soon as you can, bump into him, upset his craft and roy off. Thus a boat race is at first2and often for the greater part of the course, not a straight race after all, but a serics of -attempts at ramming. It is much more amusing to watch than the classic Oxford and Cambridge contest. _ .

The same peop:e are fond of kite flying contests where the skill of the competitors 18 employed upon' a kite. The kites are sent aloft and the game is to entangle the -string of one. kite with the string of .another, and then to saw one string over the other till one of them is broken. The winner of the wager, for here a wager takes the place of a con- ventional priz, is the man with \ his string intact..

PRESENT FOR SAPPER W. ASHTON.

Greater Responsibilities Of The

V.T.C.

Home on leave as a Sapper in the Royal Engineers, Mr. Walter Ashton, late acting adjutant to the Warrington Company of the 7th Battalion Lancashire Volunteer Regiment, visited the company's quarters at the Drill Hall on Tuesday evening, and was the recipient of a useful present, .in recognition of his past arduous services. The presentation was made at the Drill Hall. where Platoons Nos. 3 and 4 were on parade. In addition to officers of the company there were present the Mayor (Alder- man Peter Peacock) and Alderman Bennett. Making the presentation, Captain W. Peter Rylands said Mr. Ashton was very actively associated with the Volunteer movement, and upon his shoulders had fallen a great deal of the anxiety and work involved in carrying on the movement in Warrington. At the com- mencement the Corps numbered 500 or 600 men, but, through the apparent lack of appre- ciation of the Movement in the early days of the war, there was a falling away in member- ship, with great discouragement to Mr. Ashton and other active workers, including the Mayor, who had taken a very keen interest in the Corps. Times, however, had now changed. The Government and the people of the country had shown, during the last few months, a very, much higher and better appreciation of the value of the organisation. A degree of recogni- tion of a most important character had been extended, and the members might feel they were part of the recognised Forces of the Crown. In the course of- this week it .was believed that the Government would be bring- ing in a new Volunteer Bill, which would put the standard of the Volunteers upon an even higher plane. He (the speaker) had no doubt that in return for the increased recognition, and probably a capitation grant, the men would be asked to subscribe to rather more severe conditions of service, which he thought they would be only, too- pleased •to do. Mr: Ashton, he added, was a first-class man, and everybody who had worked under him in the Corps had the highest admiration for his energy, ability and knowledge of his duties. Accepting the present, which consisted of a kit bag, set of safety razors and case of hair brushes, Mr. Aslkton said Warrington should certainly have a full battalion. A regrettable feature was that more men of military age did not join the Corps. It was up to those who knew the value of the Corps to bring in out- siders.

When Men Economise.

"No, we can't afford a gardener when all Europe is engaged in a death-struggle. I'm going to cut- down that old tree for firewood, and I'll do it myself. Gimme the saw!" , Wifey gave him the saw, and he started operations. At the end of half-an-hour his efforts resulted in two logs, a brokep saw, and s'a pair of trousers sawed in half by the said saw. It was then that he came. indoors.

"I think, dear," he said, "that after all it is really selfish to do all one's own work. After all, how are the jobbing gardeners going to live if we do our own gardening? I think I'll send for old Jake." "Yes," said the tactless wife; "and at the same time you might leave those trousers at the tailor's, and see if you can get a new handle fitted to our only saw, and get a than to come round, and repaint the garden gate. The worst of this war is the way you men will econoinise."

DR. BOER AS MEDICAL OFFICER

OF HEALTH.

There was a big attendance of members at Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council, when applications for the posi- tion of Medical Officer of Health for the Council's district were considered and an ap- pointment made. The vacancy occurred owing to the lamented death of Dr. Adams.

There were five W. E. Burton, of Frodsham; Dr. H. E. Bower, Stretton; Dr. George Binns, Stockton Heath; Dr. J. S. Manson, 14, Bold-street, Warrington; and Dr. Gerald B. Murphy, Runcorn. Although all five gentlemen were nominated, one round sufficed to show a preponderance of votes in favour of one candidate—Dr. Bower— and he was accordingly declared elected. The figures were:—Dr. Bower. 24; Dr. Bur- ton, 8; Dr. Binns, 2; Dr. Manson. 0; Dr. Murphy 0.

FATHER'S LAST SERMON.

The visit of General Booth to the Warrington Hippodrome to-morrow is an event of peculiar interest, for it was in that identical building that his father, General William Booth, the founder of the Salvation Army, preached his last sermon on April 28th, 1912. "You are highly favoured to have me," he jokingly remarked •to his host on his arrival, "for it is my last campaign before I go into dock for repairs." Tho allusion was, of course, to his forthcoming operation. The General took as his text, "What shall I do then with Jesus?" At this date he was eighty-three years of age, and told his congrega- tion he was feeling far from well, and feared he,

===== 0002601_19161202_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Food Prices, Dividends, And Proposed Taxation.

The 24th quarterly meeting of the Warring- ton Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society, Ltd., was held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday. Mr. J. Upson presided.

The (suggested regulations to govern the Secondary School Scholarships were submitted to the meeting and agreed to. Upson moved the adoption of the report, which 'showed that the sales for the thirteen weeks ended October 17th were £122,401, an increase of £17,576 over the corresponding period of 1915. The membership had increased by 238, and now stood at 16,255.- lie moved the adoption of an interim dividend at the rate Of 3s. in the on general pure,kases, and is. 6d. in the .2 on the butchery department. The advisability of Paying an all-round divi- dend instead of a separate dividend on the butchery was suggested, and it was also stated that as the butchery business was so difficult to deal with the society should go out of it entirely.

The President replied to these criticisms. - Mr. Upson was re-elected president, and Messrs, Gilbert lialey, G. -P. Rushton aixl, Alfred Jones were elected. to fill three vacancies on the Committee of Management. .Mr. Green • Chappell was re-elected auditor. It was resolved to increase the remuneration of the President to £l2 per annum, and that of the committee to £lO each, _instead of the present flat rate of £5 per annum. The following special grants were agreed to: To tire National Committee for •Relief in Bel- gium, £5; International Co-operative £2; Christmas Pudding Fund. £5; National Institute for the Blind, £2 2s. It was decided to invest a further sum of £5,000 with the. Warrington Corporation. . On the motion of Mr. Wright it was resolved,: "That this meeting of members, representing 16,500 co-operators. •emphatically protests against the campaign being carried on in and out of Parliament by private traders' organisa- tions and other bodies, the purpose of which is to induce the Government to impose incoine_tax upon the savings of co-operators -obtained by their -collective trading, and further pledges its support to the committee in telly action. they may think it necessary to take to prevent charges being made upon co-operators which do not equally apply to all members of the community."

- The_ President moved,, and it was resolved "That in view of the continued increases in the cost of living. and .the impossibility of maintaining the existing rate Of dividend with- out corresponding advances in selling prices, which will press most hardly upon the poorer members, this •meeting pledges- its support to the Committee of Management in any steps they may consider it advisable to take to re- duce ,the surplus •available for the dividend by keeping down prices, and thus give the highest present benefit to the .members."

Messrs. Allen and Sinclair. who were retiring from the committee under the rules, were heartily thanked for their services.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
TO ESCAPE REPROACH.

Conscientious •Objectors Must Render

S9cial- Service.

Several men who have been to prison because of their conscientious aversion to participa-ing in war, were amongst the audience at the Friends' Meeting House, Buttermarket-street, on Sunday' evening, ,when Principal John W. pra- ham, of Manchester, delivered an interesting address entitled "War and the Social Order." The duty of Friends and conscientious objec- tors, whose blood had not been spilled by the present war, was, he said, to render social ser- vice and be foremost in the reconstruction of society after the war. They 'must oppose tariffs, for those created hostility between nations; they must labour for the abolition of poverty and lessen the gap between ric hand poor; and make the nation temperate and pure. Only by lead- ing a simpler, inexpensive and intense life, and by taking part in the vital problems Which would follow on the war, would 'they escape the reproach of having been preserved in comfort and peace, at the expense 'of the blood of their fellow citizente They must take a-greater share in Parliamentary and municipal life. War, he declared, was a cancerous growth, and they must get rid of it. He believed it was the next great evil which would be abolished, but the path would not be easy. One of the factors against the establishment of peace was the armament firms. These were of an enormous extent now, and by the coming of peace , the .fortunes of many would suffer. Mr. J. A. Ransom presided.

While walking across a mill at Whitecross Works on Saturday, a labourer named Richard 'Slater (54), • lodging at .1, Dale-street, was struck in the chest by a piece of red-hot wire which penetrated his loft lung and caused death almost instantly. An inquest was conducted by *Mr. S. Brigliguse on Tuesday afternoon. Slat,r had only been employed at Whitecross Works nine days when the accident occurred. According to Alfred Riley, of 66, lloward-street, he was going across the mill to fetch some water —.information which was augmented .by Petnr Magee, who described the • fatal incident in detail. "I didn't know. Slater was crossing," he said. "1 had .my. back to him as I received the wire coming at 420 yards a minute from thi, rolls, and when I turned round, as' it raced away to . the steam coil, .1 saw Slater .between me and the coil. I. could not possibly . stop the wire, and it entered his chest. If I had seen him a moment sooner I could•have saved lam. The Wire was'-inch in didmeter."

Mr. P. S. Steel, who repiTse-niet—l the company, 'mentioned that the mill was of a similar type to many others in the ccuntry, and at their works 'lotting of the kind had ever happened before. The .firm was desirous of protecting their workmen all they could, and were invent- ing a system whereby the wire could go into the coil from the rolls without having to touch the floor. Verdict: .1` Accidental death."

POWDERS

GENERAL BOOTH,

would not be able to speak to them with. the forcefulness he desired. But in a few moment, as if by a miracle, he became greatly animated, and Mr more than an hour his voice rang clear and strong 'throughout, the building.

Since the death of the Founder of the Salva- tion Army a great responsibility ,has rested Upon his successor. Some critics thought that the whole organisation would split and prove less effective in its social and religious work through the loss of its leader. But every critic has been falsified. To-day the Army is stronger and better equipped for its great task-. It has faced many difficulties that were inevitable at time of change and transition, but stands to- day with a greater faith in its evangel and its fine possibilities of Christian service.

When at the beginning of the war General Bramwell Booth .placed the services of the Army at the disposal of the soldiers in camps at home and on the battlefields, he was backed by Chris: tian men of .all denominations., The Army is busily engaged in catering for the needs of the British troops in England, France, Egypt and Salonica. Fifteen motor ambulances, fully staffed by Salvationists, are rendering valuable service on the battlefields in the West, and an- -other unit of five cars was recently contributed by the Salvation Army for the succour of the wounded on the Russian front, a gift which received the personal thanks of the Czar. Miss :Mary 'Booth, the General's daughter, has been working amongst the troops in France since the early months of the war, and has just published her experiences in an interesting booklet.

At the Assizes. "At Leeds Assizes, Mr. Justice Darling dis- pensed with the usual charge to the Grand Jury."—" Westminster Gazette." "More war economy."—" Punch."

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Revival of -Military Styles. In the World of Fashion the autumn and. winter of 1914 was remarkable for the sudden craze for everything Military, almost every de-, partment of women's clothes bearing witness in some form or• another to the outbreak of hos- tilities. Then the fever wore itself out, and women's clothes became eminently womanly, with adaptations from the national costumes of various Allies. This minter there is a mili- tary revival in the matter of coats, and it promises to be of no ephemeral nature, because it is sensible and suitable. The mackintosh trench coat of the Army- has been enthusiasti- cally adopted by women as the very best form of protection from the pitiless rains of this wet autumn. At present the coat is copied from the masculine model in every detail, in- __

chiding the'fleeee lining for winter.- This is not an improvement, for it gives the wearer a very bunchy appearance, but it is regarded as the distinguishing feature of the coat, and an advantage rather than otherwise. The fleecy lining is, however, removable, thus those who prefer to retain their slim proportions can do 60.

Military Belts. There is a probability that this mackintosh shape will be seen in the new spring coats for general wear, and made in covert coating, or some material suggestive of military cloths. Another unexpected development of the mili- tary, influence on feminine attire is shown in pockets, which apparently had settled down for the winter on quite womanly lines. The trench coat has brought in its train the sling and sword frog of an officer's "Sam Brown" belt. and the raison d'etre of this masculine adorn- ment is simply an inconvenient band on which to hang a flat bag. This accessory so far is of. cloth, to match the coat it accompanies; later, if it becomes popular, it may he made of leather. Fur coats are provided with very capacious pockets, and the new cloth wraps possess so many that the wearers will Probably suffer from too much zeal in this direction on the part of .the makers Of these fascinating gar- ments. It is possible to have too much of even a good thing. Winter Footwear. •

If here is at present a difficulty in obtaining sufficient supplies of children's footwear to meet the demand always great at this time of year. There will be in the near future a further withdrawal of skilled hands from this industry, but it is hoped that female labour

will be substituted, and the output maintained. Nothing approaching a famine in footwear is anticipated. Meanwhile Americans, who are experiencing a shortage of light - leathers, for- inerly -imported from Germany, -have been ex- perimenting with a sttbstitute for upper leathers, and a fabric has been produced which, when backed with adhesive cloth, has all the working qualities of real leather, -and is also as :fine and smooth as the genuine article. Prices of boots and shoes are naturally higher this season, -but there appears to be little grumbling on that account among purchasers. and indeed the :fabrics used as substitutes are so attractive, and the shape of this season's .shoes -and boots so smart—and comfortable withal—that it would be -out of place to com- plain. The Christmas displays of footwear, the war notwithstanding, will not be behind any .previous year, either in variety, good workmanship. or style. Nature a Friend.

Every 'human being comes into the world with a certain endowment of vitality or stamina.

This ,may be rapidly wasted by evil habits of various sorts, or may be conserved. The man who •succumbs to tuberculosis, pneumonia oz any other circumstance which may be regarded as a vital emergency, goes down simply because his vital ,capital is not sufficient to enable him to weather the storm. Smoking, tea, and coffee drinking, every unnatural practice consumes and dissipates the reserve fund of vitality, and when this margin of safety is used up, the body is no longer able to defend itself and succumbs. Every man who smokes, every woman who drinks strong tea, or -coffee, every gormandizer, is, through his selfish indulgence, preparing his body for a premature interview with the undertaker.—" Good Health."

Larded 'Cabbage. A piece of smoked bacon or salt -pork, when boiled with cabbages, acquires a. very pleasant -flavour. When the meat is thoroughly cooked :remove the rind, and dish with a garnish of the cabbages, first drained of moisture and chopped not too fine. Prepare, wash, and cut in two, and leave to soak for about fifteen minutes In. salted water, the hearts of four small Savoy cabbages. Boil for. ten 'minutes, then drain and lay on a. ,napkin. Take out the hard cores, add salt, and tie up the two halves together with string. Line a casserole with slices of bacon and lay the cab- bages on top. Add one earrot, one onion stuck with three cloves, and add a bunch of chopped parsley. Fitt up with broth, and finally a layer of stout, buttered paper. Place on the fire, cover the:casserole, and let gently braise for at least one hour. Then take out and strain on "a sieve, untie the., halves of cabbages, and press out in a napkin the superfluous broth. So, they can be used to garnish any piece of meat, or be ,served as side dishes with Spanish sauce.

WHAT YOU THINK OF THE' TANK.

My DEAR CHILDREN,

I have had a good many letters from you giving your opinions (ft the tank, which the British recently introduced on the field of battle. All the letters show an intelligent understanding of,the subject, showing the keen interest you must take in the Great War. As is my:, custom. when I receive -so •many letters, I -print those that win the prizes and then a few additional ideas out of the remainder, so that you may all see, what the others have been thinking. I have given two equal prizes this .week. They go to 1074 Nephew John Roylance,

13, Laira-street ; age 13; Hamilton-street School; and

===== 0002601_19161202_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
1037 Niece Nancy Adams,

27, Samuet-street; Evelin-street. School. Several of my nieces and nephews have wished me "A Merry Christmas," for which I thank them. I hope they will have a happy Christmas, too. It struck me that that subject would be a nice one for the next competition, which shall be a compgition on The Kind of Christmas Party I Would • Like to Attend.

Nephew Thomas Atherton writes to jell alto he is leaving school this week, and asks whe- ther he .must resign his membership .of the Corner. I have never been able to say when children:cease to be children. It is a question for themselves. I think we should .be glad to keep all our members as long as those members' feel tiutt they would like to remain among us as. children.

Your loving

UNCLE JACK.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WINNING LETTERS. By Nephew John lioylance.

"Tanks are /enormous machines, which have. been known-to crawl over, a trench and capture it in a very short time. They are -practically irresistible. They went to France from Eng- land as tanks; hence they -derive the name. When they got on the battlefield they were fitted up as instruments of war. When they are in action I believe they can push trees away like match-stalks. There was a man who helped to drive a tank and he said: The finest achievement done, in my .opinion, with our tank was-demolishing a sugar factory.' The Germans ran away like terrified rabbits, but the Kaiser said: ' You are not fighting .fair.' The tanks have a lace resembling a dragon and have guns pointed .out at almost every possible angle, and when they fire the Germans

think that it is a dragon which Spits fire. Tlkey have proved themselves to be a most dangerous invention, and are certainly, as regards Ger- man opinion, a most remorseful engine of death."

===== 0002601_19161202_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
By Niece Nancy Adam

"Tile tank, in my opinion, is the greatest invenfi on and infernal war machine ever thought of. It is staggering the world as to its coolness in going into action. It takes ev'erything so cool, lets the enemy examine it, lets them fire and bomb. They can make neither head nor tail of it. It opens, to my Idea, its port holes and blows anyone or any- thing around it to atoms. I for one would like to be one of the boys inside to think that after all the cowardly work the enemy have done to the poor Belgians, girls and others, we should be paying them in return. -If I could help in making them, every soldier should have one roll on the tank, or the Caterpillar Crawler,' as I have heard it called, but I do not know whether it is true."

===== 0002601_19161202_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD IDEAS FROM OTHER LETTERS.

Niece 'Ethel 'Shaw, Sunnyside, Chester New- road (Bolton).—" From what I have read of the tanks, I think their real value will be. in get- ting to the enemy trenches and destroying the machine guns, 'which, in every charge, have caused such terrible loss to our brave soldiers. If they can do nothing else than this they are worth their weight in gold." Niece Sarah Holmes, 1, Hamilton-street (Hamilton-street School).—" The thickness of the steel is 8 inches and shells can do no harm. to it."

'Nephew J. H. Singleton, 24, Gandy-street.--'- "Tanks have terrorised the .German soldiers so that thousands have surrendered, thus. les- sening considerably the enemy forces opposed to our men, which makes victory easier." :Nephew Jed( Grundy, 8, Roome-street (Bea- mont School).—" I am- glad .it is a good old British invention."

Nephew Frank Overend, 74, Lythgoes-lane.— " A tank is a huge monster which travels over the firing line to make the enemy, with all its forces, retreat." Nephew William Watkins-on, 7, Heaton-street (Hamilton-street School).--" This great land- Dreadnought is of great use ,to Great Britain and her Empire. Nephew John Ditchfield, 55, Orford-laue.--"A tank is a very powerful armoured car, with very powerful engines and wheels, which allow it to run anywhere it likes." Nephew Robert Shaw, 8, Leonard-street (Hamilton-street School).—" Everything they come in contact with they destroy, but the sooner the better they come in contact with the Kaiser."

Niece Emily' Marshall, 183, Winwick-road, thinks we should give Mr. Winston 'Churchill some 'honour to show that we appreciate his invention—one of the greatest inventions ever made.

Niece Emmaline Spencer, 58, Winwick-road (Silver-street School) says if the tank could take prisoners the war would not have lasted so long. Nephew Frank Holmes, 1, Hamilton-street.— "The tank is a steel structure, which, a few years before the war, was used for tilling the hard ground of South America. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1916.

NEW BOOKS.

RED CROSS AND IRON CROSS.

Crimes For Which Germany Will

Suffer Here And Hereafter.

"Red Cross and Iron Cross"" is a graphic book of certain phases of the war written by a doctor in France who believes the day will come when the civilised world will pick out the criminals from the barbarians and punish for infamies committed by a savage foe. The documents for accusation , furnished by the accused themselves reveal a sombre study, and prove that the responsibility falls on the leaders and not on, the men. The English doctor had strayed into a village near which fierce fight- ing had • been taking place. There were wounded of French and German, nationality, and the village church had become the hospital, with the obsolete old village doctor in charge. He welcomed the stfanger as a gift of Provi- dence. The stranger doctor had lost all his; kit, possessing nothing but a morphia syringe in his pocket, whilst the old village doctor had neither morphia, chloroform nor anaesthetics of any kind. There were side by side on the straw- covered floor more than a hundred badly wounded soldiers. Here and there blood had trickled down upon the straw an& turned the marble floor into a slippery red. Upon such a scene Of human misery the British Red Cross doctor strayed. Tell-tale Groans and Ignorance.

One of the patients was an alleged British soldier. Uniformed in a complete British uniform the doctor suspected him from the first. When the alleged Englishman awoke the doctor proffered him a cigarette. The wounded soldier declined with thanks. "Can't a Woodbine tempt you?" said the doctor. "What?" asked the soldier.

"A Woodbine. You don't mean to say that you don't know what a Woodbine is? If so you are the only man in His Majesty's Ex- peditionary Force who doesn't know." Later he confessed he was a spy, and respon- sible for the death and wounds of all the men in the church. "Did you," said the doctor, "secure your khaki greatcoat by killing your man? , Or did you rob the dead hyena fashion?" When dressing his wounds the doctor replied when he groaned: "No, Fuchs, when a clever mail gets himself up nea 'Tommy' he ought to know that an Englishman never groans when the doctor dresses his wounds. He clenches his teeth if it comes to the worst—but that is all." . A Hideous Confession.

Another of the wounded was a German Major. Roaring in anger because no morphia was in the doctor's possession, he wrought himself into fury. He trembled with rage until he could find no words in French to express himself. Point- ing to the German soldiers, he said, "lbo was a superior officer, and that he must have a room to himself and could not be mixed up with his men," and then shouted in his rage a word in German which led the wounded German soldiers to look up angrily. The Major died of tentanus. "I am glad he is dead," said the soldier. "I have lived in fear of him night and day as my squadron commander. He has kicked- me often, the last time he struck me with his whip across the face was the day before I was wounded." Rising upon his wallet of straw the soldier said to the doctor, " Did you hear what he called us in his anger. He called us Schweine (swine)—that is how a Prussian officer speaks to his men. We obey them, cowards as we are, because we fear them; but we hate them as much as we fear them. It was he, who made us shoot the women and children at Dinant. It. was he who made us loot and plunder Termonde. I rolled off to sleep that night with a bottle of champagne in my hand on the steps of the high altar in one of the churches." _ _ _

Such and similar narrativesmake thebook vivid reading. It helps us-to realise the horrors of an invasion by troops who, goaded on by their superiors, perpetrate acts of barbarity and savage cruelty. , The author's profits on the sale of the book will be handed over to the French Red Cross.

"Red Cross and Iron Cross." By a Doctor in France. ' London: John Murray, Albernarle-street, W. 2s. 6d. net.

The Wave. An Egyptian Aftermath, hy Algernon Blackwood. Macmillan and Co., Ltd., London.

One naturally looks for something weird awl strange in Mr. Algernon Blackwood's books. This book is no exception to the rule. The story deals mainly with three English people who are together in Egypt. No country could be better fitted for the display of that mysticism and sub-consciousness in which the author de- lights. Two of the characters have been in the habit of seeing visions and dreaming dreams from their childhood, and at Luxor, surrounded by the memorials of a long dead greatness, they become vividly conscious of an intimate personal connection with that far-off time. To the ordin- ary reader, this book will be the most inter- esting of any that Mr. Blackwood has written, for the characters are such as enchain one's in- terest to the very end of the story. Other readers who have psychical leanings, will find much to command their sympathies, apart from the mere thread of tlw story. And a smaller number, perhaps, who are neither psychically inclined nor mere novel readers, but who know something of Egypt, will be charmed with the descriptions of that strange land. Mr. Black- wood is no ordinary, writer. 'lt is a pleasure to read such well-written books, and we grate- fully welcome the latest• effort of the autlicir.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTH LANCS.' " DAY."

Bowled Over The Germans By The Dozen. •

• South Laneashires' "Day." Played absolute hell with the enemy.. Had to attack position running around a strongly-fortified village. All approaches swept by machine-gun fire. Artil- lery raining big shells on ground all the time. Souths were splendid. They fought their way right up to the village under a deadly hail of fire. They carried the first line of trenches and got into the village street, where they were supported by a tank.. Remains of houses had been fortified by the enemy. From behind the ruined walls machine-guns kept peppering the Lancashires.. They advanced steadily never- theless, and gave the enemy rough time of it. This was the first time the foe had seen a tank in that region. They thought they could try all sorts of tricks with it. They came out from their cover while the South Lancashires were engaged in clearing out some cellars and tackled the tank. It was an exciting time. The tank opened fire at once, and the Germans were bowled over by the dozen. The rest scooted for cover like a lot of frightened hares. When they got back into their underground lair they found a nice surprise awaitino. them. A party of Lancashire lads had mad their way through from an adjoining cellar which they had cleared, and they immediately opened fire on the Germans seeking shelter from the tank. Enemy were completely taken by -sur- prise, and surrendered, though they outnum- bered the Lancashire party by four to one.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE'S FINE STAND.

The Cheshires were on the job. Were furiously attacked while holding a trench cap- tured from the enemy the day before. All the old tricks were tried in succession. Liquid fire and poison gas, and then a furious bull-rush. The Cheshires flung back each attack, and gave the enemy a hot time generally. Then ,attacks ceased and there was a furious shellinefor hours followed by another attack made with great fury by new troops. The Cheshires smashed this attack, 'and pressed home a counter-attack.-7 From an °officer's description of the recent fighting on the Somme.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
RELIGION.

An Analysis Of Conflicting

Theories.

The publication of this work is opportune. The group theory of religion has for some years past been rapidly gaining ground, and there was need for some criticism of its assumptions and an attempt at adjustment 'between its claims and the claims of the individualistic view.

iMr. Webb, in this volume, submits to a fairly searching examination the Theories of the Nature of Religion put forward in " L'Aunee Sociologique" by the French school, whose acknowledged leader is M. Emile Durkheim. The views of M. Levy Bruhl, whom Mr. Webb regards as the chief of M. Durkheim's colla- borators, are subjected to a particularly keen analysis. M. Levy Bruhl attempts to explain the nature of Religion by an ingenious theory, worked out in a most elaborate manner, based on the alleged difference between the mental operations of primitive men and of ourselves. "The development of the intellect in individuals is 'conditioned by their social background, and the source of our notions of time and space, of cause :and substance, is to be sought in what are called 'collective representations.'" But ,certain other, notions, among which are included those relating to religion, have reference to no other objects than to such collective representa- tions. That is, in M. Levy Bruhl's view, there is no objective reality to which these notions which form part of all 'religious theory refer. Hence religious belief, of whatever kind, is illusion and will eventually give way before the advance of scientific exactitude. N. Emile Durkheim parts company, appar- ently, with his collaborators, at certain points of the theory; and has indeed varied consider- ably, with the course of time, in his own views on the matter, his latest definition of religion being a decided advance from his former posi- tion and that of his colleagues. _ _ .

- Mr. Webb has little diculty, we think, in rebutting the view of these writers that the mind of primitive man works in a radically different Manner from ours. If this view were correct, it is difficult to conceive how the development from that stage to our own has been effected without the hypothesis ,of supernatural inter- vention, and that would naturally be ruled out at once by the French School of Sociologists. Nor do we think Mr. Webb is less happy in his criticism of the use these writers make of the theory of "collective representations." Their whole theory of the nature of religion seems to be based on psychological 'views which are very far from meeting with general acceptance and which seem indeed to be, not only largely assumptions, without a vestige of evidence in support, but actually erroneous.

- , Mr. Webb makes some reference to the theories of the origin and nature of religion, not dis- similar to those of the French school, put foe- ward by Miss Harrison and Mr. Cornford, but he shows that the reasons for dissent from the former hold good also in regard to the latter. .The outstanding defect of these group theories, in Mr. ,Webb's view, is that they reduce so enormously the value of the individual and in- deed whittle away his position until he is lost in the social whole. Mr. Webb, on the con- trary, while not underestimating the social element in the life of the individual, desires, if anything, to emphasise the personal and in- dividual element in. Society. This part of his book, if we may say so without disrespect, is much the more interesting. It is to be regretted that the conditions under Which the book was written—it is based on lectures delivered under the Wilde Trust—have compelled Mr. Webb to restrict himself to an examination of the views of the French Sociological School. Mr. Webb's work would, we think, have been greatly enhanced in value had he been enabled to extent his inquiry so as to include a consideration of some of the philosophical theories which have soinething in common with the groin) theories, but to which he 'only allude briefly in a note. Perhaps in a subsequent mirk Mr. Webb will complete the task begun in the preSent volume which, meanwhile, we may very heartily com- mend to the increasing public which interests itself in these matters.

*" Group Theories of Religion aisl the Religion of the Individual." By Clement C. J. Webb, M.A. London: George Allen and Unwin, Ltd. tis. net.

FARM AND GARDEN.

YOUNG PIGS.

Mr. S. F. Edge writes: It is a very bad prin- ciple to buy newly weaned pigs and take them into new surroundings. In my early days of pig keeping I quickly learnt this, and now we never sell a little pig under 12 weeks old, because I never think it gives real satisfaction to the buyer. It is a most uneconomical proce- dure buying little pigs at 6 weeks old, and they never grow into really good pigs. With my pedigree pigs, to get them to grow into really good pigs I think it well to keep them on their mother until 10 or 12 weeks old, but feed them well all the time as Soon as they Can eat, so that they practically become'self weaned, so that the actual weaning is neither a shock to them nor the mother. This, coupled with my open air system, ensures a most extraordinarily hardy pig, which will make an extremely rapid fatten- ing Porker or baconer, or if a pig kept for breed- ing it means one with a good and robust consti- tution.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
RAPID MILKING.

It is an undoubted fact ,that the person who milks the cow has much to do with, not only the quantity of milk she gives, but also the quality. Cows have strong likes and dislikes regarding those who milk them, and some cows will never take kindly to certain persons. In one experiment, says "Farm and Home," one milker in one week sticceeded in obtaining over 211 b. more butter-fat than (lid the other milker who followed him, though there was but a slight difference in the amount of milk obtaited. One great secret in milking is to work rapidly, and to do the work smoothly and without any tugging and jerking. Most cows like to be milked by a skilful milker, but few like to be " fooled " with, and the milking process become a long spun-out

stripping. When once begun, let the milk draw- ing go on without cessation. To stop and begin again is not allowable. Become an expert in the art. Milk-giving is a function far more allied to the nervous system than once supposed, and to get the best results there must be quiet conduct when among the cows, doing nothing to raise resentment or excite them to more than ordinary conditions. When for any cause the cow holds up her milk she does so from the fact that she is exercising a nerve control of the 'milking glands, sad to resort to any further nerve-exciting cause is to defeat the little in- fluence one has left. The only way is to in some agreeable manner call her attention to some other thing, such as an appetising meal, and then, while she is consuming it, finish the milking. Milk rapidly, do not hurt • the cow, milk cleanly, milk with both hands until done; when finishing, reach well upon the udder, get the last drop, And then :itup.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
"NUT" CAKES AND MEALS.

LOtd Crawford desires to draw the attention of farmers to the importance of ascertaining, when purchasing nut cake or nut meal, the kind of nut from which the , cake or meal is made. Formerly, earth-nut or ground-nnt .(Arachis hypogea) cake or meal was the prin.- cipal cake or meal made ;from nuts and used as food for cattle, and the terms " nut " cake and " nut " meal were generally und'erstood as applying to this nut. Recently, however, coconut and palm kernal residues have come into common use. Since the outbreak of war other nuts have been introduced, and, as these new materials become available for feeding live stock nut-cakes and nut-meals of several kinds are likely to be found oh the. market, which may be expected to vary considerably in their

composition, character and uses. Farmers should, therefore, insist on having all such cakes and meals described on the invoice by a name clearly marking their origin.

WOMEN IN AGRICULTURE.

The President of the Board or Agriculture desires to call public attention to the urgent need that exists for the assistance of women, not already connected with agricultural try, in the work that is required for food pro- duction on the land, and to replace agricultural labourers who have been called up for military service. Hundreds of women have already. rendered valuable service in maintaining the home-grown food supply, but thousands are now needed to meet tho national emergency. Educated women are especially invited to offer their services and short courses of training can be provided for them. Application should be made to the secretaries of the • WOmeres War Agricultural Committees in the various counties, or to the Women's National Land Service Corps, 50, Upper Baker-street, London, N.W.

ALLIED FARMERS' CONFIDENCE.

Next to the confidence of the .soldiers of the -Allies optimism is nowhere greater than among the 'peasant farmers who, as each "push" of the troops is maao on the Western Front, follow it up and seek to place them- selves afresh on such of •their holdings as are available for cultivation. French women and children and .old French people have thus suc- ceeded in maintaining the standard of culti- vation right up to within a few yards of the firing line. In the Marne and the Meuse dis- tricts they have been enabled to do this in a large measure by the gifts of implements, live stock, and seeds made by the Agricultural Relief of Allies Committee (16; Bedford- square, London, W.C.). The committee 'continues to w",tch the course

of military operations on all -Fronts and to consult the Governments of our Allies with a view 'to stepping in with practical farming help for these brave peasant folk the momint the opportunity occurs.

IN THE GARDEN.

THE LOGANBERRY.

All allotment holders and gardeners should grow this 'very fine fruit it is the finest thing yet received from America. It can be trained on a fence or upon a pillar, being similar in growth to a blackberry. The loganberry is a natural cross between a raspberry and an Ameri- can blackberry, and grows very vigorously. The fruits are very large, of slightly acid flavour, and grow in bunehes ;:its chief value lies in its excellence us a. preserve. The fruit is produced on previous year's canes, which should be securely tied to wires or pegged down. They can be got from nurserymen at the cost of 6d. or 9d. each. Prune after planting- to secure good canes for next season._ .

===== 0002601_19161202_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
GARDEN RUBBISH.

A common Complaint at the present time is that no manure can be had, and however freely artificial manures may be used they cannot take the place of farmyard or horse manure. This holds good more especially where the soil is devoid: of humus, hence it is all the more necessary to fall back on leaves, waste garden 'matter, and vegetable refuse of all kinds to add to the soil.. In a rubbish heap there is, of Course, the possibility of weed seeds, but these can be destroyed, as also any insects that may

be present if some lime is mixed with the bulk, adding any dung that may be available. The heat generated by the manure will go far to prevent the germinatioh of the weed seeds. For a clayey soil the above mixture is very suitable, helping to mollify and break it. down, while if the soil is light it Will be rendered more retentive ' of moisture . while the 'necessary humus will be added to it.. If, as .very- often happens, there is no--cpace or, convenience for treating the refw;,o in the way referred to, then it is an easy matter to make what is known as a smother lire and gradually burn it, the ashes therefrom being valuable in that they contain potash so necessary for many garden crops. -

INCREASING BUSH FRUITS.

• • it is always advisable to be. "aye strickin' in" a few cuttings of bush fruits, as gooseberrieS,• red, white and black currants, so. that there May be -a -few young, Vigorous plants to fill up any blanks, to replace old bushes- that have got old, and cease to bear, or it may be make a new plantation. When making the cut- tings, which should be about 12 inches long, of gooseberries, and red and white, currants, re- move all the buds with the exception-of three or four at the . top, OS if this is, neglected a crop of suclers will. result. By doing this the, plants have a clean stem, and thee i then no danger of a crop of suckers. In the case of ,black- currants this removal of the buds is not necessarY, as in their case .it is all-important that the young,growths should spring from the, base' of the plant and thus allow for the cut- ting out of the old wood. - The black 'currant fruits on the younr, wood in contradistinction to the red and wriite varieties that fruit on the spurs. If • the position. selected for the cuttings is at all heavy then it is advisable to put into the bottom of the trench some sandy 'soil for the cuttings to rest on. After the cuttings have been put in ' fill in With the ordinary soil and Make the whole firm.

FORCING RHUBARB.

If any reader has some vell-established clumps of rhubarb it is an easy matter to cover one or two of these with an old flour barrel or box, and cover , with a mixture of stable manure and leaves—the leaves to pre- ponderate ton distance of two feet on all sides beyond the Chimps selected, with from a foot to eighteen inches of the same material over the top of the box. Care must be taken that an opening is, left at the top of whatever is used as a covering to allow of the escape of rank steam: To - prevent the leaves blowing about it is well to place some Jittery material over. them. If the above is done at once it is quite possible to have some forced rhubarb for the, Christmas dinner.—" Wick," in "Farm and 'Home."
