A PICTUR.T.SED ROMANTIC NOVEL BY ROY LMcCAIZDEIL ADAPTED FROM THE SENSATIONAL -FLYING K SERIAL FILM BY ALEC J. BLAID.

[Characters in ihis story are purely imaginary, living

CHAPTER XXVII.

MINE OWN PEOPLE.

Forgetting the injury to his leg incurred when escaping from gaol, Luke Lovell fled out of the Italian boarding house and through the garden with the diamond in his possession. Even after she had brought another lamp to the charming signor's room, Signora Solari was excited. ,Infect, she broke out into loud cries of "Assassins!" "Robbers!" and "Police!" and are she could be restrained had rushed to the doorway overlooking her wine garden and made the night hideous with her cries. Luke Lovell: pausing under a light in the empty street some distance off to reassure himself he had the diamond again, heard her screams and hid the jewel once more and slipped off through the shadows. Two blocks away two policemen, pausing to wait for their midnight relief, also heard the signora's cries. But when they reached the establishment Solari the, Signora had been quieted by the counsels of her more composed daughter. They explained to the police that there had been a quarrel between two of the patrons—a quarrel that had greatly alarmed the signora and her daughter, since the cafe, as the gentlemen of the police knew, was a most respectable place. It was fated that the il3-s4arred jewel would lose two guests for the Cafe La Bella Napoli, for, roused to his danger were he caught, being tired of confinement and feeling sure the evil repute of the diamond was well founded, De Vaux had hurriedly cast off the clothes he had been wearing in hiding, and donning his usual and more pleasing attire, the diamond thief, the last of "the pack," dropped from a back window of the lodging and made off down a deserted alley through the night. In his slumbers in the hayloft Quabba was aroused by the presence of Luke, and in the moon- light that streamed through the open window of the. loft Luke showed him The Diamond from the Sky. Quabba crossed himself and made the sign that fends off the evil eye. He had come to believe the great diamond of the Stanleighe was the amulet of the evil one, perhaps the evil eye itself, who knows? "You are to take it," said Luke. "Take it to Virginia to our mistress Hagar and our little mis- tress Esther. It belongs to them. I do not want it now; I only want revenge. When I have that, too, will return to Virginia and serve Hagar faithfully again. Till then you take the diamond and be gone. You have the money the English lawyer gave you for your return and the shipment of the non's- back. Give me the price to ship back the pony, and while you are gone I can for a while be a simple organ man with his monkey, as you were. Who will think to loci'," for me as such?" •" No, no!" shuddered Quabba. "I do not wish -to have the evil diamond."

"It will not harm you." said Luke, as super- stitious as the hunchback. "The Diamond from the Sky is only evil to those who thi,l •to evil. Your heart is good.': But Quabba, was loth to do so. He was not so sure, poor fellow, that his heart was good. But Luke forced the great jewel upon him. Then Quabba had further objections to the plans pro- posed by Luke. "It is well what you say," he ventured, "all except Clarence. We could not be parted,' could we,; Clarence, my son? We were parted once for a time, and his little heart nearly broke by reason of it," and the monkey, as if he sensed his master's words, whimpered and clung to him. "Well, take the monkey with you, then," said Luke. "It may be all the better, for the monkey is known as your other. self ;.while as for the pony and the piano cart, what does if matter? One pony cart and one pony of the sort is much like another, but a monkey—there is something that people will remember."

So at dawn Luke, in the guise of a limping, itinerant musician, drove the pony and the piano cart out from Los Angeles to a distant place, and in time shipped the outfit back by express to Quabba, at Fairfax, near to which town was the gipsy rendezvous. Then Luke hid out in the wilds as ?, tramp workman known to the confraternity as "a blanket stiff," and worked at such jobs as he could get, biding his time till the search for him as an escaped gaolbird would have ended and he could return to Los Angeles for his revenge on Arthur, Blair Stanleigh and Frank Durand. For Luke did not know the last-named suave interna- tional crook had met his death over the diamond. When Quabba took the train accompanied by Clarence, the monkey, he travelled in the smoking car. Fate takes us separate ways, yet sometimes we travel closely with those whose concerns are ours, and we never know. In the Pullman jour- neyed the dapper and furtive De Vaux. For the most part he kept his face behind a newspaper and hoped to be unseen, so he siw not. For several thousand miles De' Vaux, formerly accomplice and jackal to Durand, travelled in fear of the police and the vengeance of John Powell and the desperate Blair Stanleigh—and all the while the priceless diamond, for which he and his dead master had risked so much, travelled on the same train in the rags of a poor hunchback with a monkey. To'. Stanleigh Hall shortly afterwards 'came a hunchback gipsy with a monkey clinging fondly to him. "I have," he said to Hagar and Esther, "come from Luke Lovell with The Dintond from the Sky." "For all the money in the world I would not keep the diamond," said Esther. The bewildered mesfiteiger could not understand such an attitude, but he philosophically watched the packing of the jewel. He saw it addressed to John Powell and then he posted it. So the months passed. To keep Arthur from thinking, to keep him engaged that ihey might further enmesh and despoil him, Blair and Vivian led the so-called John Powell from one dissipation and wasteful luxury to another. Arthur's strong constitution battled with the drug habit and the other dissipations more or less successfully. But his will had grown weak and his better self silenced—all of which was beheld by his loyal man-

ad have noliconnection with Sr relation to any non.]

iiervant Parker. the one faithful friend remaining to him, with misgiving and foreboding. The mysteries of the past rose before Hagar and Esther. The proud Virginian families would have nothing to do with them. Hagar realised that while she stayed with the girl she loved so dearly the position would remain the same. If the 'truth were told. Esther would no doubt be received as the Stanleigh heiress. Once her true status was known, the girl's future would be bright and happy. This could only be done if Hagar went back to her own people.

One night Hagar made the venture. Her things were secretly packed; her silent gipsy help pre- pared for her departure in the night. Esther slept, and -Hagar crept silently by her bedside to pray and leave a note that would tell Esther of her de- termined resolve. But Esther stirred and woke at the light departing footfall of Hagar, and finding the written message, caught the weeping Hagar at the portals of the old mansion while yet her car- riage waited. In loving struggle Esther bore her back to the ,library that had seen the great tragedy of their lives.

"You shall not leave me. I will die; I will kill myself if you do," cried Esther, throwing herself into the arms of Hagar, while the tears welling from these two loving steadfast hearts gave bitter savour..to the parting. But Hagar was resolved. "Thu dieadful'lie—this living lie of twenty-four years and more—must die," said Hagar, resolutQlY. "Our ways lie different. The wrongs of years I can undo at the breaking of my heart, my dear child."

" And• at the breaking of mine," cried Esther, "What are the Stanleighe to me? What have they done for me? What will they do for me? What will they do for you? I would rather beg my bread by your aide throughout the world than dwell with- out here or anywhere in every luxury that could be proffered me. I swear you shall not leave me.", Hager sobbed, yet was resolute. "You are my mother in place of her who died when I was born. A tender mother through all the happy years we dwelt together before ambition and desire for things that are vain parted us and brought the sorrow to our hearts that now we feel," sobbed Esther. "I will not let you go, or, if you go, I Will go with you."

Again she flung herself into the arms of Hagar, who, though her own tears blinded her and her sobs choked her, endeavoured to calm the shuddering, heartbroken girl. Absently fingering the Bible on the table before them, Hagar, opened it at the Book of Ruth. "This shall be an' omen and a portent," she whis- pered. With eyes scarce seeing the twain read that old sweet inspired message from one loyal woman's hea:rt to another.

" ' And Ruth said, Entreat me not to leave thee, or to return from following after thee. For whither thou goest I Will go; and where thou lodgest I will lodge. Thy people shall be my people, and thy God my God.'" A deep hush fell upon them. Then Hagar spoke. "It is God's will." she said.

That night these two, never again to be parted, closed the great door of the gately house behind them and journeyed on in a dTep, silent happiness to where the humble gipsy people awaited them. "Strike camp," was Hagar's command when the wild and happy gathering of the wandering folk at the return of their beloved queen and princess had calmed. She pointed westward. No further word was said, but day after day, month after month, the caravan moved ever westward, and Esther knew they journeyed,to seek*Arthur and to save his soul and bring him back from the living hell wherein he dwelt.

• • 4 • • • • • The secret Arthur kept. Neither Blair nor Vivian were aware that The Diamond from the Sky had come back to him. It arrived at the offices in Bleir's absence, and the secretary sent Dick Jones, one of the clerks, to the Powell mansion with the packet. With it was Hagar's message: "0, child of my heart, not a diamond but a lov- ing mother's prayer is the true 'charm against harm.'"

Locked in the library. Arthur had read this mes- sage. Unmanned and weakling as he now was, he felt that the diamond was sanctified by this prayer of a heartbroken mother of whom he was un- worthy. Though Vivian had woven a spell around him, he felt in his secret soul it would be sacrilege to place the diamond around her fair white throat ter it had been blessed by a prs,yer, even though he was lost too far to heed it.

It was then that the first wild idea of self-de- struction crossed Arthur's disordered mind. He secured a deadly poison and hid it in the safe in the library together with the diamond and its wrappings and the message from the heart of his mother—the heart he knew he had broken. Know- ing he had wronged Blair and kept him from his birthright, a deep regard for Blair, wicked as he know him to he, had grown in Arthur's heart—like a weed where there should have been flowers. Dimly, loyally, he trusted Blair—trusted and loved him—and was',not man enough to tell the truth now that the truth had grown a living lie through all the years.

But disillusionment was coming fast. Parker, his man, had been promised the revelation of a great secret one day. Vivian's maid had often hinted at a secret she held over her master's head, and Parker, affecting to succumb to the wiles of the none-too-prepossessing maid,, waited till she divulged it. Then she showed him the marriage certificate of Blair and Vivian.

At that moment the secretary of ' the Fairhope Oil Cornbany" waa announced.• He brought proof that Blair had diverted a sum of no less than two hundred and forty-eight thousand dollars to his .personal account. By various ways he had almost crippled the Powell enterprises, and Arthur saw that only hard work could save the situation. Was he capable of the needed effort

For a moment hope flickered. Perhaps, after all, he could make good, restore his good name, and win Esther. Hope as quickly died. Parker handed him the marriage certificate he had borrowed from the maid.

This was the end of it all. He had been tricked, deceived, and robbed. That very day he would drink a toast to death. ,

LOST IN LAST MINUTE.

Warrington Lose By A Penalty Goal

===== 0002601_19161202_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
At St. Helens.

By a penalty goal scored against them in the last minute of the game. Warrington were beaten by St. Helens on the latter club's ground on Satur- day, the final score being five points to three. The visitors were without Brookes and Young, but Jolley made a welcome re-appearance at full-back, and gave a fine display.

_ Warrington Non the toss, and St. Helens started against the wind and rain, and immediately domin- ated the game. From a scrummage in the centre of the field the Warrington forwards broke away, but Wilson cleared, and Green'all and White, seconding his efforts, the ball was carried to the Warrington quarters, where every effort was made to get through, Ashall being brought down on the line. Jolley touched down, and from the kick-out the Warrington forwards made a capital rush to the other end, where Jones was tinlneky to lose the ball after a good run. From the ensuing scriinmage the home team cleared, but Warrington almost im- mediately returned to the attack, and Baker made a bold bid for the line, but Greenall tackled strongly. Smart footwork by the visitors' forwards saw the home line again assailed, Douglas and Pot- ter making strenuous efforts to capture the citadel. The home defence, however, prevailed, while a shot at goal by Jolley was unsuccessful.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
I:l2shlng Play.

White and Hahall changed the scene of opera- tions, but Jones received and made a fine dash for the visitors, but was brought down in magnificent style by Wilson. The nest incident ,of note was a Ana passing bout by Potter, Tranter and Baker, but the St. Helens tackling left nothing to be desired. A similar round of handling among the home backs, might have got them out of a dangerous position, had not Ashall fumbled the ball. Heaton and White made a good effort for St. Helens, and took play to the half-way line. The home threequarters kept up the pace, and might have scored, but for Ashall kicking too bard. Baker being able to touch down. A• few minutes later Heaton lacked speed ,when favourably placed. White then dribbled well for the home team. but Tranter returned with a big kick. Immediately afterwards Potter, Trainer and Horrocks combined, and took play to the St. Helens line, but lost the ball. Here Jolley put' in a cross kick, and Greenall fumbling the ball, Baker dashed up and scored in the corner. Jolley failed to con- vert. Half-time: Warrington, one try (3 points); St. Helens, nil.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WINNING GOAL.

Early in the second half St. Helens exerted pres- sure, but Jolley was safe in defence. The visiting custodian made a good save from Farrimond, fol- lowing which the Warrington threequarters paid a visit to the other end. T,ranter sent the ,ball out to Baker, who made a plucky attempt to get through, but Greenall tackled well. Following an attack by the home threequarters. Ashall cross- kicked, and Aspinall, dashing up, scored a fine try, which White failed to improve. Warrington now attacked strongly, and, Baker made several com- mendable efforts to get through, but was not suc- cessful. In the closing minutes of. the game St. Helens worked their way to the Warrington quar- ters, and Wilson tried a drop at goal, but was un- successful. Soon afterwards the home team were awarded a penalty kick, and Ashall landed a fine goal, which gave his side the victory. Result: St. Helens, one goal one try (5 points); Warrington, one try (3 points). The teams were:—

St. Helens: Wilson; Greenall, White, Ashall, Aspinall; Heaton, Halsall; Daniels, Jones, Farri- mond, Brighton, Tomlinson and Sephton. Warrington: Jolley; Baker, Tranter, Potter, Hor- rocks; A. Jones, Douglas; Harmer, Green, Cunliffe, Matthews, Wall and J. Jones. Referee, Mr. C. Jameson, Wigan.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
JOLLEY IN HIS OLD FORM.

On the St. Helens side, Ashall played a fine game in his unaccustomed position at full-back. Aspinall worked hard on the wing, and White was a good centre. Heaton did well at half- back, while the forwards were best represented by Farriniond, Brighton and Jones. As we have previously stated, Ben Jolley played a great, game for Warrington. His field- ing was clean, and he kicked a good length and with judgment. Baker and Tranter were promi- nent threequarter-bicks, while Jones was a nippy half-ba'ck. The forwards all played a good, robust game, and were much smarter than the opposihg set.

ASSOCIATION.

THE TOWN CLUB ON THEIR NEI\ GROUND.

On Saturday the Town Association Club played their opening match on their new ground at St. James' Field, The Causeway, before a greatly increased number of spectators, amongst whom were a good sprinkling of wounded soldiers. Their opponents were the Altrincham team, and owing to the keen rivalry which exists between the teams no better fix- ture could have been wished for. The visitors could not turn out with a full team, and Connor, a member of the Town Club, made up their eleven. Lloyd, who has gained a reputa- tion' with the Everton team, was expected to assist .the locals, but did not turn out, and Siddall was also: absent, Midge and 'Owen filling the vacancies. The game opened in a spirited fashion and some keen exchanges were witifessed in mid-field. The visitors were the more prominent with fine passing movements in mid-field, but failed to pierce the rolvist defence of the home team and the first half was goalless.

The second portioli opened very fast and keen, Johnstone and Best defending stoutly for the visitors, and Connor was prominent in leading the attacks on the home goal. The visitors were successful in drawing first blood, a shot by Cope was only partially cleared, and Daniels rushed in and sent the ball into the net. Following this, the home team stormed the visitors' goal and shot l after shot was repulsed, and the defence finally prevailed and transferred play to mid-field. Then several futile attempts were made on the home goal, and Leah was at his best form. Time was drawing near, when the home, forwards again attacked keevly, and after Woodhouse had skimmed the bar with a good shot he male amends by eluding Johnston and c,entreing nicely in front of goal. Illidge nipped in and headed a capital goal, thus equalising the scores, and no further goals were registered at the cOll of time.

The visitors were best represented by :Wallin- son, Johnston, Best, Connor and Daniels, and the pick of the home team were Leah, Whit- field, Woodhouse and Midge.

Altrinehani have not yet been defeated at Warrington.

Connor's display last week entitle,s him to more consideration from the home club's committee.

The Town Club should receive better support now they have got a ground nev the centre of the town.

All football enthusiasts will now be able to enjoy the Saturday afternoon Causeway .walk.

It is a pleasure to see the keenness with which the wounded soldiers follow the game.

It was a disappointment to many that Lloyd did not turn out.

In the Junior League Howley United just managed to gain the verdict after a keen struggle.

The Hornets are playing some close games in their away fixtures, which, with ground advantages, should give them victories when playing on their own soil.

Latchford Cadets journeyed to the Tanhouse Lane ground, but had a relapse and were easily defeated by three clear goals.

The Howley United forwards appear to have lost the art of decisive action since Woodhouse left them. There was a lack of shooting ability against the Hornets.

Widnes St. Maiy's '-.surprised themselves last week. To beat Orford Juniors on their own ground is worthy of congratulation.

Doughty, Austin and Witton were the best of the team. Buckley and Chisholm left their shooting boots at home.

Atherton, Broome, McAllister and Gregory were the most prominent for the "Saints."

The Orford Club have captured a good local junior who will greatly strengthen their .attack.

The Gra,ppenhall Lads did not rise to the occasion in their " Derby " fixture with the Recs.

The game was keenly contested up to the equalising goal being scored.

Aikenhead was unfortunate in putting through his own goal.

Rigby played a fine game at full-back for Grappenhall. Jones, Willoughby and Knight also did well. Lockhart shows improvement since his trial with the Town Seamd.

Brandwood, Green and Wilcox also played well for the Recs.

A very uneven match was witnessed on the Penketh Recreation Ground. In the first -half

the home team held the advantage of four goals against one. In the second half they dis- played a very confident snirit and the visitors realising this very quickly scored a couple of goals and nearly- suceeeded in making the scores equal. It was only by strenuous play in the closing stages of the game that the home team retained their lead.

The monthly meeting of the Junior League will he held on Monday, night.

Played on the Recreation ground. In ten Minutes Pearson, by a breakaway, was enabled to open- the score for the home team. NOTABLE NAVAL DISASTERS.

The dliasters that have occurred to the vessels of the World's fleets have by no mean! all occurred in batee. The sinking -of.a ship, the death of a screw, are things that we al- most exp:-Tt in the turmoil of actual conflict, and though they bring with them a sense oi personal loss to many who have no.relatibtc in/the fight, -yet there is. after all, somethinz less terrifying about these adversities than ii the terrtlile disasters that &cm occasionally in. t-imes of peace. It is the object of .this • ar- ticle to draw attention to a few of the most terrible catastrophes that have occurred in the last century or so.

;"—A-pr-11-2-6-111, —lBBl, a vessel, the "Doter- el " blew up in the Straits of *Magellan, and Of the hundred and fifty ga:lant tars who were on board at the time, only seven were saved. No one was ever ab:e to explain the origin of the exTilosion. Some of the experts, who were afteAvards. consulted, supposed that it might have Ten eine to an explosion of 'coal gas, but a more novel explanation was also forthcoming. It appeared that a new kind of explosive paint was being tried on the vessel, and the suggestion was made that some of this paint had leaked, had run under the magazine door, and so caused the loss' of the vessel and the deaths of a hundred and forty-three brave nica. A MISTAKE IN JUDGMENT.

In the year 1892 a thrill of horror /went through the land at the news that the " Cam- perdown " had rammed and sunk the "Vic- toria," the flagship of the Mediterranean fleet, off Tripoli. On that shin there were 642 men. Of these 336 sailors and 23 officers, including the admiral, Admiral Tryon, sank to the bottom, of the sea. °wino. to a mistake in • judgment,- and a wrong movement; a. huge ship was lost and suffering spread into hun- dreds of homes.

A. situAar accident befell the "Van- guard," but fortunately on this occasion there was no loss of • life. The " Vanguard " had cost half a million of money, and was regarded as one of the finest vessels in the British navy. She was cruising off .Wicklow with another huge ship, the Iron Duke." There was a slight fog at the time, but no- .thing of any great consequence. The admiral suddenly altered his course with the inten- tion of passing across the stern of the "Van- guard." This'. latter ship, however, mistook the intention of the "Iron Duke," and slack- ened speed. The consequence was that the admiral's ship crashed into her consort, and though • she sustained little damage herself, her ram tore right through the vessel with which she had collided, and sent her to the bottom* within half .an hour.

, In 1870, or thereabouts, a new type of ship was invented which was officially, christened the "'Captain," but which was nicknamed by old tars, the "Coffin,': and a coffin she proved. She had no keel, and was, the first and last of her type. Off, she went on a cruise with nearly five hundred men on board.. When opposite Cape ,Finnisterre she was struck by, a squall and capsized. Down she went in three minutes. At. the moment of the. disaster there were only a few. men on deck, of whom eighteen were saved. The rest were buried al.ive in the patent new steel coffin.

===== 0002601_19161202_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO TERRIBLE ACCIDENTS.

The ",Thunderer " was the scene of two terrible accidents. First of all her boiler burst, and nearly a hundred people were killed, half of whom were scalded to death. Three years later one of her guns burst dur- ing a firhig practice, killing ten men and wounding :thirty or forty others. Another great catastrophe took place in home waters, in full -view of the English coast and excited possibly more sympathy than if it had occurred elsewhere. It was one of the most sudden, appalling and over. whelming of all the disasters on record. The Eurydice" was a wooden sailing frigate, which was used for training young seamen for the British navy. She. had been for a long cruise in foreign waters, and was anxiously expected home. On the day of .her expected- arrival the weather turned cold and squally, and threatened to dela:o the home- coming.. At last the ship was sighted by the coastgvpard, making. her way grandly -under all sail. " and with a tine breeze toward; the anchorage at Spithe.ad . . . . But .suddenly a black cloud driving. before the sharp breeze discharged ?itself upon the sea in a furious outburst of wind and snow; for some time the whole face of the waters was blotted out from view, the air was choked- with fierce squall and blinding snow. When at length it cleared away the "Eurydice" was no longer to be seen. Caught unawares by thtt savage blast, she had instantly capsized, and of her crew of some 300 officers and young seamen, but three lived to tell the tale. Boats hurried off from the island to render what aid they could; fishing ves4.ls in the offing put their helms -up and ran down to the .scene of the disaster, and it was one of these which picked up the three seamen Who alone .survived. Some two or three more were alive when first taken' out of the water, but died from expo- Sure and exhaustion ere they could reach the shore."

The next year , a similar fate befell the "Atalanta," another training vessel. She was lost out of sight of land there was not a sinee survivor and the'mystery of. her fate has never been solved.

'THE LOSS OF THE "ROYAL GEORGE." With another story of sudden loss we have been familiar from our earliest days, that of the loss of the "Royal George," an incident rendered into English verse of singularly grapaic quality by the poet Cowper. Even .at this distance of time, and with much of the. freshness of the 'story worn away by our early acquaintance with Cowper s verses we scarcely escape a thrill when we think of. the terrible event. The "Royal George" was the first ship in the navy of the day. It had been the flagship of Hawke, .and of many brave commanders. Just before going on a new expedition a small leak was discovered. In order to stop this the l'essel was laid on her side. So little -fear of danger was thene about such an operation that the admiral and all the officers remained on board. The decks were also crowded with women and children and visitors who were paying Calls to their friends. It is said that .at the moment of the disaster there were at least three hundred women on board. Everything was calm and peaceful. The visitors were . mostly between decks: the admiral was writing in his cabin. The carpenters, eager to get their task fin- ished. inclined the ship a little more than they had been ordered, and at the very mo- ment when they had got her into a danger- ous position, a sudden squall. of wind arose, threrir the ship upon her side, and her gun- ports being open, Ak e instantly filled with water and went down. A- smaller vessel lying 14ar was swallowed up in the whirlpool caused by the sinking of so large a ship, and several yet smaller craft, though a con- siderable distance away were put in About. three hundred—chiefly sailors—were able to save themselves by swimming or by the boats, but the number who perished amounted to close upon, a thousand. A DISASTROUS NIGHT.

It is doubtful whether so manv disasters have ever been crowded into so short a space of time as those which occurred on the night of the "Great Storm'. in 1703 when, so it is reported, over a thousand ships of different classes were lost. Amongst the British men- of-war that met their doom were the "Stir- ling Castle" of 70 guns, the "Mary ': of 70 guns. the "Northumberland" of 70 guns, which went down on the Goodwin Sands, the "Vanguard" of 70 guns, that was lost near Harwich, and out of whose 'crew of 300 only four were saved. The. " Re'solution "'orti down off the coast of Spithead, losing to. guns and 173 men, the "Newcastle" of 60 guns went down in Spithead,. and most of lies brave tars perished in the sea. We may hope. as Hamilton Williams says in his "Britain's Naval Power," "that as science expands day by day and puts fresh possibilities in our power, it will enable us to safeguard thoroughly, from rocks and temp- est, fire and foe, and unforeseen fatality, the great navy on which at this present time, fat 'noire than evdr before, the wealth, safety, mi strength of the kingdom chiefly depend. -

Accumulating Flour.

It has beej). reported to the Board of Trade that a certain number of persons have been ,buying •considerable quantities of white flour, with a view to accumulating a sufficient stock for private consumption to enable them to aVoid for several months the effects of the recent order. The Board of Trade therefore think it desirable to remind such purchasers that on January 1,1917, it will become illegal. for them to make use of flour which does not comply with the order for the preparation Of bread or any other article of food without the express author- ity of the Board. Such authority will only be given in exceptional circumstances.

Nunn Pudding Alatinee.

A. splendid monster variety Matinee attracted. a great audience to the Hippodrome on Thurs- day afternoon, when un exceptionally fine pro- gramme was specially arranged for the occasion by Mr. W. A. Jackson, meager, with the object .of providing puddings iland Christmas. cheer for the soldiers. • The fiin of the after- noon was a grand auction sale of. an extraord- inary variety of articles, Mr. John Mainwaring being an ideal auctioneer. The first lot was fine plum ,pudding,, which was sold several. .times and realised ,t 2 18s. 6d. for the Terri- torial --.Equipment Next Wednesday night only one performance will be given, commencing at seven o'clock, and at its conclusion there will be a special auction sale on behalf of the same fund. Mr. Main- Waring will offer from 100 to' 160 lots, the articles being of. a varied description.

Druids Iteeognise Bro. Hankey's Work.

On Saturday night an interesting ceremony was performed at Alte_Crown and Sceptre, the meeting-place of the Druids' - Camp Lodge. when the services of Bro. James Hankey, P.N.G.A., were recognised • by the presentation of a framed emblem of the Order. The pre- sentation was made by District Grand Master Arthur J. Shaw, on behalf of the Lodge, At a very appropriate manner. In the course of his' speech be pointed out the good feeling and fellowship Friendly- Society work induced into every-day life. Bro. W. Woods, P.J,D.G.M., also spoke on behalf of .the distriot, endorsing the words of Bro. Shaw, and hoping Bro. Hankey would still lend a band to the officials of the Lodge. Bro. S. Packer, P.D.G.M., secretary of the Lodge, spoke with regard to the great assistance Bro. Hankey. had been to him in carrying out the duties of the Order. District Treasurer Bro. R. Perdue. P.D.G.M., and the Lodge Treasurer, Bro. E. B. Worrall, P.N.G.A.,-also added their testimony to the esteem in which Bro. Hankey was held. Bro. Hankey, in accepting the emblem, thanked the D.G.M. and all present for the kind words spoken. He hoped to be able not only to have reached the highest office of the Lodge, .but of the district in time. A vote of thanks was passed to Bros. Shaw and Woods for their attendance.

FARMERS' SONS. Government Urged To Keep Skilled Labour On Land. SUBSTITUTES DISCUSSED. The question of the proposed military sub- stitution of farm hands is causing great con- cern on the part of the farmers, who are wor- ried about the efficient production of food, and at a nieeting in the Corn Exchange Room, Horsemarket-street, on Wednesday afternoon, a large number of them protested against the fur- ther diminishing of skilled 'hands, and, besides sending a resolution to the authorities, ap- pointed a deputation of four to go to London and present their case. Mr. Edwin Marsh, of Grappenhall, who pre- sided, said that if this country were to allow the skilled men to go off the land, the country was, without a doubt, going ,to be faced with severe trouble. Skilled mea on the land were fulfilling very big—positions, far greater than any they could fill in the Army. Mr. Robert Allen, of Culcheth, said that if the Government carried out their scheme, and took away' farmers' sons and other skilled hands, by this time next year people would be lucky if they got a meal. Farmers' sons, he said, were worth more than six substitutes. He resented the attacks on the farmers because of the high prices obtaining, contending that no other people worked so hard for so little money. (Hear, hear.) The following deputation was chosen to see the authorities in London: Messrs. R. Allen (Culcheth), James Mercer (Halewood), Edward Marsh (Grappenhall), and S. Gould (Lymm). Alderman Bennett was present at the meet- ing, and, in response to the invitation of the Chairman, made an interesting speech, in the course of which he declared that there was not a more patriotic lot of men than farmers. •

the following resolution: "That every man of military age who is granted exemption be told that he must join a. Volunteer Training Corps, and that all men who -have been already ex- empted shall be told to join also, and that local drill centres he provided for the convenience of the men on farms." GERMAN PRISONERS. Suggested Use Of Them On Local Farms. There. is a possibility of German pris,riers being employed on local farms. At the last meeting of the Warrington District War Agricultural Committee, Mr. J. Cawthorne, lion, secretary, 'reported that Lord 'Wolmer, M.P., had written, stating that when he ap- proached the Board of Agriculture regarding the depletion of labour on farms in this district, they replied that they are doing all they can to safeguard the interests of farmers, but they do not hold out much prospect of getting addi- tional labour, except in respect of prisoners of war. His Lordship added: "Now I should very much like the Warrington and District War Agricultural Committee, to consider the ques- tion of prisoners of war MostOarefully. Times are immensely difficult, and we have got to re- sort to new methods to meet our difficulties. . . . The whole of their (German) farm work is now done by women and prisoners, and men over the age of 55. If you can. tell me how many German prisoners you think you can em- ploy, and how many your neighbours can em- ploy, I will spare no* effort to try and get you them." During the debate the opinions of those pres- ent were widely divergent, but after much study of the I,ituation it was realised that an increased production of food is an imperative necessity. The Secretary was instructed to inform Lord Wolmer that the committee is not at all averse to his proposals, but .the first consideration is flint before any supplementary labour can be fully utilised, regular and skilled workers must be also employed.

Notices of births, marriages, and deaths, in memoriam notices, and acknowledgments of sympathy are inserted in "-The Examiner" at the following rates :—Not exceeding 20 words, 1/6 each; every additional seven . words, 6d.

Marriages. BRACECAMP—DAY.—At St. Paul's Church, War- rington. on Thursday. November 30th, Frank William Bracecamp (Second. Lieutenant R.F.A.), to Joan Marion Crundall Day.

Deaths. FULTON.—On November 27th, at 24, Fletcher- street, Elizabeth Fulton, aged 48 years. i KNOX.—On November 24th, at 12, Beresford-street, Thomas Knox, aged 78 years. WOODS.—On November 26th, at 23, Amelia-street, William Woods, aged 70 years. WORRALL.—On November 28th, at 69, Watkin- street. Martha Worrall, aged 58 years. In Memoriam. DAVIES.—In loving, memory of Company Sergt.- Major Thomas Skidmore Davies, killed in Belgium November 28th, 1915. "Death cannot divide." From his loving WIFE & MOTHER. DAVIES.—In affectionate remembrance of Company Sergt.-Major Thomas Skidmore Davies, of the South Lancashires, who was killed in Belgium, November 28th, 1915. • If I could have raised his dying head, Or heard his last farewell. The grief would not have been so hard For those who loved him well. Often my thoughts do wander To the grave so far away, Where they laid my precious brother Just a year ago to-day. From his loving SISTER & BROTHER-IN-LAW (Florrie and Tom), of Watford. Acknowledgments. MRS JOHN BOOTH and FAMILY tender their sincere TII.A.NKS to all friends for their kind expressions of sympathy in their sad bereave- ment; and for their floral tributes. 45, Bewsey-street, Warrington. RIFLEMAN WM. MACE and the MISSES MACE desire to gratefully ACKNOWLEDGE the sympathy shown in their sad bereavement: also to thank those who 'sent floral tributes.

Mr. Marsh Succeeds the Late Mr. Britch.— At Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, the Chairman (Mr. Antwis) offered a cordial welcome to Mr.- Edward Marsh, who had been co-opted a member of the Council in the room of the late Mr. Edwin Britch. Mr. Marsh is chairman of the Parish Council of Appleton.

CHEQUE FOR £4O. Presented To Rev, J. M. Buck- master On His Departure. About to leave St. George's Mission to become Vicar of St. Andrew's, Wigan, the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster was presented with a cheque for .21.0 by the parishioners assembled in the Mis- sion Schoolroom on Monday evening. Among those present were the Rev. and Mrs. Buckmaster, the departing Curate's 'Parents-. Mr. R. Sumner, who handed the cheque to Mr. Buckmaster, said that one point which had been very much appreciated was his visiting. Not only in the day time, but at all hours of the night he was often at the houses of .his people. The rev, gentleman was also instrumental in starting the funds for the chancel, which scheme, however, had, owing to the war, been put on one side. The smaller scheme of the •building of the porch, however, was undertaken, and with that they would always associate the Rev. Buckmaster's name. The altar had also .been improved, but he (the speaker) knew that Mr. Buckmaster would be the last man in the world to take the credit for these things. The Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) said that during the last few months ho had been called upon to suffer two very great losses—Mr. Vickers from St. Clement's, and Mr. Buckmaster from St. George's. lie had always looked upon his clergy as his sons and as his brothers. He had always trusted them implicitly, and had never found his trust misplaced. Mr. Buckmaster had been to them not only a preaching, but a teach- ing priest, and that was what every Church needed, From St. George's this year there were over one hundred candidates for confirmation— a result never before achieved. Canon Morley Stevenson, the Rev. T. Vickers and the Rev. S. G. Stanton added their tribute, the latter remarking that Mr. Buckmaster had put before him some high ideals to which he might aspire. His life bad been one of untiring industry, with One aim before him—that of glorifying God. The Rev. T. M. Armour said that in Mr. Buckmaster's life there was theory and prac- tice united. Mr. F. M. Darby said the keynote of Mr. Buckmaster's life had been what all Christianity ought to be—self-sacrifice, and be would ask them to go forward in the way which had been so splendidly shown them. Replying, He Congratulates Others. Replying, the Rev. Buckmaster said he felt as if they .were heaping coals of fire on to his head. It was not he who had done this good n'ork, but his people. He bad not deserved half they had said about him. He congratulated Walter Buckley and Harry Peake, two members of the choir, who had won the °Military Medal. The choir, Sunday school teachers, workers, and children had been wonderful. He would never get others like them.. He thanked everybody, .especially the Rector, Mr. and Mrs. Buckley, and the representative warden, Mr. Sumner. . Three cheers were given for Mr. Buckmaster, and the audience`sang "For he's a jolly good fellow." A musical programme was given during the evening by members of the church. CHURCH LADS' BRIGADE. Archbishop's Encouragement And Benediction. His Graec the Archbishop of Canterbury has sent the .following greeting To the Church Lads' Brigade, on the occasion el its twenty- fifth anniversary : " I should like, in this twenty-fifth year of tho life of the brigade, to send to its members a word of encouragement • and •benediction. Throughout England and across the sea we have watched the growth of .the brigade and noted the good work which :its members have been enabled to do. In the tremendous strain of the war the brigade has manfully taken its place and borne a worthy part on behalf of a sacred cause. Those who in that great and terrible enterprise have laid down their lives for their country are held by us in grate- ful remembrance, and, their example will con- tinue in years to come to inspire our thoughts and strengthen our .endeavours. May . God have the men and lads of the brigade, past and present, in His keeping day by day, and enable us 'shoulder to shoulder to go forward with His help from strength to strength." Lucky Dogs. PRONTIONS AND DISTINCTIONS. PRIVATE, A. BERRY. Thrice Wounded Man Receives The Ali Mary Medal. Warrington's proud ..record of :military honours is augmented again by the addition of the name 'of Lance-Corozal .• Albert Berry, South Lancashire Regiment. - who received his decoration—the, 'Military Medal—for services rendered ow September 25th. Tie resides, in peace times; with his wife and one child at 10, Rosewood-avenne. Lance-Corporal I.ierry enlistr-d on October 14.

' ..06.1.4414, • 11,:von LANCE-CORPORAL A. BERRY Military Medal.

1914, crossed to France on February 12th of last year, and has been wounded three times. He formerly worked at the Dalton Bank Works of Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd.. lie was edu- cated at the National Schools and attended St.. Clement's Mission Sunday School. • Two brothers are serving—Fred, of the A.S.C, joined in October, 1914, and was invalided home after nine months' service in France; Robert, of .the Cheshire Regiment, is in train- ing. One brother-in-iaw, a nephew and twelve cousins are serving in different branches of the Army. . _ AValking Sticks for Wounded Soldiers. Colonel Simpson, the administrator at the Lord Derby War Hospital, writing to Mr. Stewart Royston, Sunnyside, Paddington, says: "The large parcel of walking sticks came: to hand yesterday. There are 55 of them and they are a very good collection... They will be very useful indeed and very much appreciated by the sick and wounded soldiers here." We are requested by Mr. Stewart Royston to say that he will welcome gifts of 100, 500, or even 1,000 walking sticks for our wounded "Tommies" and "Jacks "—or cheques or money orders to buy them for -our suffering heroes.
